-kernel of a morphism corresponds to a standard notion: the stable monoid, defined in
Straubing 2002
. Given a morphism
\alpha:A^{*}\to M
into a finite monoid, it can be verified that there exists a number
d\geq 1
such that
\alpha(A^{2d})=\alpha(A^{d})
. The least such number
d\geq 1
is called the stability index of
\alpha
. The stable monoid of
\alpha
is
N=\{1_{M}\}\cup\alpha(A^{d})
. One may verify that
N
is the
MOD
-kernel of
\alpha
(this follows from a simple analysis of
MOD
-separation).

Clearly, having a
\mathscr{G}
-separation algorithm in hand suffices to compute the
\mathscr{G}
-kernel of an input morphism
\alpha
. This yields the following lemma.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a class with decidable separation. Given as input a morphism
\alpha:A^{*}\to M
into a finite monoid, one may compute the
\mathscr{G}
-kernel of
\alpha
.

We now characterize
\mathscr{G}
-kernels in terms of
\mathscr{G}
-orbits when
\mathscr{G}
is a
group
prevariety.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a group prevariety and let
\alpha:A^{*}\to M
be a surjective morphism into a finite monoid. Let
N
be the
\mathscr{G}
-kernel of
\alpha
. The two following properties hold:

•

Every
\mathscr{G}
-orbit for
\alpha
is a subset of
N
.

•

N
is exactly the
\mathscr{G}
-orbit of
1_{M}
for
\alpha
.

Proof.

First, let
e\in E(M)
and let
N_{e}
be the
\mathscr{G}
-orbit of
e
. We prove that
N_{e}\subseteq N
. Let
q\in N_{e}
. This yields
s\in M
such that
(e,s)
is a
\mathscr{G}
-pair and
q=ese
. By contradiction, assume that
q\not\in N
. By definition, we get a language
K\in\mathscr{G}
separating
\{\varepsilon\}
from
\alpha^{-1}(q)
. That is,
\varepsilon\in K
and
K\cap\alpha^{-1}(q)=\emptyset
. We exhibit an element
x\in K\cap\alpha^{-1}(q)
, yielding a contradiction. Since
K\in\mathscr{G}
, Proposition
2.4
yields a
\mathscr{G}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to G
recognizing
K
. Moreover, Lemma
3.5
implies that
G
is a group since
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety. Since
(e,s)
is a
\mathscr{G}
-pair, Lemma
5.3
yields
u,v\in A^{*}
such that
\eta(u)=\eta(v)
,
\alpha(u)=e
and
\alpha(v)=s
. Let
k=\omega(G)
and
x=u^{k}vu^{k-1}
. Since
\eta(u)=\eta(v)
and
G
is a group, we have
\eta(x)=(\eta(u))^{k}=1_{G}=\eta(\varepsilon)
. Hence, since
\varepsilon\in K
and
K
is recognized by
\eta
, we have
x\in K
. This a contradiction since
\alpha(x)=e^{k}se^{k-1}=ese=q
and
K\cap\alpha^{-1}(q)=\emptyset
by hypothesis.

It remains to prove that
N
is exactly the
\mathscr{G}
-orbit of
1_{M}
for
\alpha
. Since we already proved that the latter is included in the former, it suffices to show that every
s\in N
belongs to the
\mathscr{G}
-orbit of
1_{M}
. Since
s\in N
, we know that
\{\varepsilon\}
is not
\mathscr{G}
-separable from
\alpha^{-1}(s)
. Since
\varepsilon\in\alpha^{-1}(1_{M})
, it follows that
\alpha^{-1}(1_{M})
is not
\mathscr{G}
-separable from
\alpha^{-1}(s)
,
i.e.
,
(1_{M},s)
is a
\mathscr{G}
-pair. Hence,
s=1_{M}s1_{M}
belongs to the
\mathscr{G}
-orbit of
1_{M}
, as desired.
∎

5.3.
Characterization

Let us first recall the definition of aperiodic monoids. We use an equational definition, specific to
finite
monoids. We say that a finite monoid
M
is
aperiodic
when every
s\in M
satisfies
s^{\omega+1}=s^{\omega}
. We are ready to state the generic characterization of
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. In fact, the statement also mentions the correspondence with
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
. This is important because we still have to prove the inclusion
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
, and the argument is intertwined with the proof of the algebraic characterization.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a prevariety and consider a regular language
L\subseteq A^{*}
. The following properties are equivalent:

(1)

L\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
.

(2)

L\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
.

(3)

All
\mathscr{C}
-orbits for the syntactic morphism of
L
are aperiodic monoids.

Before we prove Theorem
5.11
, let us discuss its consequences. First, it yields a transfer result concerning the decidability of
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-membership. It follows from Lemma
5.6
that the
\mathscr{C}
-orbits associated to a morphism into a finite monoid can be computed as soon as
\mathscr{C}
-separation is decidable. Hence, we obtain an algorithm for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-membership in this case. Given an input language
L
, one first computes its syntactic morphism
\alpha:A^{*}\to M
. Then, one computes all elements
s\in M
belonging to a
\mathscr{C}
-orbit for
\alpha
(this is possible since
\mathscr{C}
-separation is decidable). Finally, it follows from Theorem
5.11
and the definition of aperiodicity that
L\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
if and only if every such element
s\in M
satisfies
s^{\omega+1}=s^{\omega}
.

Corollary 5.12
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a prevariety with decidable separation. Then,
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-membership is decidable.

Theorem
5.11
can be simplified in the special case of classes
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
where
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety. In this case, it is possible to characterize the languages in
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
using only the
\mathscr{G}
-kernel
of their syntactic morphisms. Indeed, we have the following statement as an immediate corollary of Theorem
5.11
, Lemma
5.10
and the definition of aperiodic monoids.

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a group prevariety and consider a regular language
L
. The following properties are equivalent:

(1)

L\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
.

(2)

L\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{G})
.

(3)

The
\mathscr{G}
-kernel of the syntactic morphism of
L
is an aperiodic monoid.

Remark 5.14
.

Schützenberger’s original characterization
Schützenberger 1965
of the class
\mathit{SF}
of star-free languages is an immediate consequence of Corollary
5.13
. Indeed, consider a regular language
L
and let
\alpha:A^{*}\to M
be its syntactic morphism. Since
\mathit{SF}=\mathit{SF}(\textup{ST})
, it follows from Corollary
5.13
that
L\in\mathit{SF}
if and only if the
ST
-kernel of
\alpha
is aperiodic. Moreover, since
\textup{ST}=\{\emptyset,A^{*}\}
and syntactic morphisms are surjective, it is immediate that the
ST
-kernel of
\alpha
is the whole syntactic monoid
M
. Hence,
L\in\mathit{SF}
if and only if its syntactic monoid
M
is aperiodic. This is exactly Schützenberger’s theorem.

Proof of Theorem
5.11
.

We fix a prevariety
\mathscr{C}
for the proof. Moreover, we let
L\subseteq A^{*}
be a regular language and
\alpha:A^{*}\to M
be its syntactic morphism. We already proved that
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
in Theorem
4.10
. Hence, it suffices to prove that
(1)\Rightarrow(3)
and
(3)\Rightarrow(2)
. The latter implication corresponds of the inclusion
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
, which we omitted in the proof of Theorem
4.10
.

Implication
(1)\Rightarrow(3)
.
Assume that
L\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. For every idempotent
e\in E(M)
, we prove that the
\mathscr{C}
-orbit of
e
for
\alpha
is aperiodic. By definition, this boils down to proving that for all
s\in M
such that
(e,s)\in M^{2}
is a
\mathscr{C}
-pair for
\alpha
, we have
(ese)^{\omega+1}=(ese)^{\omega}
. The argument is based on Proposition
3.3
. By hypothesis on
\mathscr{C}
, it follows from Proposition
3.2
that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
is a prevariety. Hence, since
L\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
, Proposition
2.3
implies that its syntactic morphism
\alpha
is an
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-morphism. Consequently, Proposition
3.3
yields a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to N
such that for every
u\in A^{*}
, if
\eta(u)
is idempotent, then we have
(\alpha(u))^{\omega+1}=(\alpha(u))^{\omega}
.

Since
\eta
is a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism and
(e,s)
is a
\mathscr{C}
-pair for
\alpha
, Lemma
5.3
yields
u,v\in A^{*}
such that
\eta(u)=\eta(v)
,
\alpha(u)=e
and
\alpha(v)=s
. Let
k=\omega(N)
. Clearly,
\eta(u^{k-1}vu^{k})=\eta(u^{k})
is an idempotent of
N
. Hence, it follows from the definition of
\eta
that
(\alpha(u^{k-1}vu^{k}))^{\omega+1}=(\alpha(u^{k-1}vu^{k}))^{\omega}
. Finally, since
\alpha(u)=e
and
\alpha(v)=s
, this exactly says that
(ese)^{\omega+1}=(ese)^{\omega}
, as desired.

Implication
(3)\Rightarrow(2)
.
We assume that all
\mathscr{C}
-orbits for
\alpha
are aperiodic monoids and prove that
L\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
. We first present a preliminary definition. Given a language
K\subseteq A^{*}
and an element
s\in M
, we say that
K
is
s
-safe
when
s\alpha(u)=s\alpha(v)
for every
u,v\in K
. Additionally, given a language
P\subseteq A^{*}
, an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
P
is a finite partition of
P
into languages which all belong to
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
. The argument is based on the following lemma.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
P\subseteq A^{+}
be a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay such that there exists an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{H}
of
P
whose elements are all
1_{M}
-safe. Then, for every
s\in M
, there exists an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
whose elements are all
s
-safe.

We first apply Lemma
5.15
to show that every language recognized by
\alpha
(such as
L
) belongs to
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
and conclude the main argument. By definition,
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
is closed under disjoint union. Hence, it suffices to show that
\alpha^{-1}(t)\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
for every
t\in M
. We fix such an element
t
in
M
.

Note that
A\subseteq A^{+}
is a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay. Moreover, the set
\mathbf{H}=\bigl\{\{a\}\mid a\in A\bigr\}
is an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
A
such that every
H\in\mathbf{H}
is
1_{M}
-safe. Hence, we may apply Lemma
5.15
in the case when
P=A
and
s=1_{M}
. We get an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
such that every
K\in\mathbf{K}
is
1_{M}
-safe. Being
1_{M}
-safe means that
\alpha(K)
is a singleton for every
K\in\mathbf{K}
. Therefore,
\alpha^{-1}(t)
is the disjoint union of all
K\in\mathbf{K}
intersecting
\alpha^{-1}(t)
. Since
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
is closed under disjoint union, we obtain that
\alpha^{-1}(t)\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
, which concludes the main argument.

It remains to prove Lemma
5.15
. Let
P\subseteq A^{*}
be a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay and consider an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{H}
of
P
such that every
H\in\mathbf{H}
is
1_{M}
-safe. Moreover, fix
s\in M
. We need to build an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
such that every
K\in\mathbf{K}
is
s
-safe. We proceed by induction on the three following parameters listed by order of importance:

(1)

The size of
\alpha(P^{+})\subseteq M
.

(2)

The size of
\mathbf{H}
.

(3)

The size of
s\cdot\alpha(P^{*})\subseteq M
.

We distinguish two cases depending on whether the following property of
s
and
\mathbf{H}
holds. We say that
s
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable
when:

(4)

\text{for every $H\in\mathbf{H}$,}\quad s\cdot\alpha(P^{*})=s\cdot\alpha(P^{*}H).

The base case happens when
s
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable: we conclude directly without using induction. Otherwise, we use induction on our three parameters.

Base case:
s
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable.
This is the only part of the proof where we need the hypothesis that all
\mathscr{C}
-orbits for
\alpha
are aperiodic monoids. Lemma
5.3
yields a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to N
such that for every
u,v\in A^{*}
, if
\eta(u)=\eta(v)
, then
(\alpha(u),\alpha(v))
is a
\mathscr{C}
-pair for
\alpha
. We define,

\mathbf{K}=\{P^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)\mid t\in N\}.

Clearly,
\mathbf{K}
is a partition of
P^{*}
. Moreover, it only contains languages in
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
. Indeed, we have
P\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
: it is the disjoint union of all languages in the
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{H}
of
P
. Therefore,
P^{*}\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
since
P
is a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay. Hence,
P^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
for every
t\in N
since
\eta^{-1}(t)\in\mathscr{C}
(as
\eta
is a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism). It remains to show that every language
K\in\mathbf{K}
is
s
-safe. The argument is based on the following fact, which is where we use the hypothesis that
s
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable.

Fact 5.16
.

Let
q,f\in\alpha(P^{*})
such that
f
is idempotent. Then, we have
sqf=sq
.

Proof.

The proof is based on the following preliminary result. For every
u,v\in P^{*}
, we show that,

(5)

there exists
r\in\alpha(P^{*})
such that
sr\alpha(u)=s\alpha(v)
.

We fix
u,v\in P^{*}
for the proof of (
5
). There exists a decomposition
u=u_{1}\cdots u_{n}
with
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in P
. We use induction on the length
n
of this decomposition. If
n=0
, then
u=\varepsilon
and it suffices to choose
r=\alpha(v)\in\alpha(P^{*})
. Otherwise,
u=wu^{\prime}
with
w\in P
and
u^{\prime}\in P^{*}
admits a decomposition of length
n-1
. Induction yields
r^{\prime}\in\alpha(P^{*})
such that
sr^{\prime}\alpha(u^{\prime})=s\alpha(v)
. Moreover, since
w\in P
and
\mathbf{H}
is a partition of
P
, there exists some
H\in\mathbf{H}
such that
w\in H
. Since
s
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable and
r^{\prime}\in\alpha(P^{*})
, it follows from (
4
) that there exists
r\in\alpha(P^{*})
and
x\in\alpha(H)
such that
sr^{\prime}=srx
. Additionally, recall that
H\in\mathbf{H}
is
1_{M}
-safe by hypothesis. Hence, since
x,\alpha(w)\in\alpha(H)
, we have
x=\alpha(w)
. Therefore,
sr^{\prime}=sr\alpha(w)
. Altogether, this yields
sr\alpha(u)=sr\alpha(w)\alpha(u^{\prime})=sr^{\prime}\alpha(u^{\prime})=s\alpha(v)
, which concludes the proof of (
5
).

It remains to prove the fact. Consider
q,f\in\alpha(P^{*})
such that
f
is idempotent. By definition, there exist
u,v\in P^{*}
such that
q=\alpha(v)
and
f=\alpha(u)
. Hence (
5
) yields
r\in\alpha(P^{*})
such that
srf=sq
. Since
f
is idempotent, we obtain
sqf=srff=srf=sq
, which completes the proof.
∎

We are ready to show that every language
K\in\mathbf{K}
is
s
-safe. By definition,
K=P^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)
for
t\in N
. Given
u,v\in K
, we have to show that
s\alpha(u)=s\alpha(v)
. Let
n=\omega(M)
and
e=(\alpha(u))^{n}\in E(M)
. Since
u,v\in K
, we have
\eta(u)=\eta(v)=t
. Hence,
\eta(u^{n})=\eta(vu^{n-1})
. By definition of
\eta
, it follows that
(e,\alpha(vu^{n-1}))=(\alpha(u^{n}),\alpha(vu^{n-1}))
is a
\mathscr{C}
-pair. Hence,
e\alpha(vu^{n-1})e
belongs to the
\mathscr{C}
-orbit of
e
, which is aperiodic by hypothesis on
\alpha
. This yields
(e\alpha(vu^{n-1})e)^{n}=(e\alpha(vu^{n-1})e)^{n+1}
. Multiplying by
s
on the left gives
s(e\alpha(vu^{n-1})e)^{n}=s(e\alpha(vu^{n-1})e)^{n+1}
. Since
n=\omega(M)
, we know that
(e\alpha(vu^{n-1})e)^{n}
is an idempotent of
M
. Moreover, since
u,v\in K
, we have
(e\alpha(vu^{n-1})e)^{n}\in\alpha(P^{*})
. Therefore, Fact
5.16
yields
s(e\alpha(vu^{n-1})e)^{n}=s
. Together with
s(e\alpha(vu^{n-1})e)^{n}=s(e\alpha(vu^{n-1})e)^{n+1}
, this yields
s=se\alpha(vu^{n-1})e
. We now multiply by
\alpha(u)
on the right to get
s\alpha(u)=se\alpha(v)e
(recall that
e=\alpha(u^{n})
). Finally,
e
is an idempotent of
M
and since
u,v\in K
, we have
e,\alpha(v)\in\alpha(P^{*})
. Hence, we may apply Fact
5.16
twice to get
se\alpha(v)e=s\alpha(v)
. Altogether, this yields
s\alpha(u)=s\alpha(v)
, as desired.

Inductive step:
s
is not
\mathbf{H}
-stable.
By hypothesis, we know that (
4
) does not hold. Therefore, we get some
H\in\mathbf{H}
such that the following
strict
inclusion holds,

(6)

s\cdot\alpha(P^{*}H)\subsetneq s\cdot\alpha(P^{*}).

We fix this language
H\in\mathbf{H}
for the remainder of the proof. The following fact is proved by induction on our second parameter (the size of
\mathbf{H}
).

Fact 5.17
.

There exists an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{U}
of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
such that every
U\in\mathbf{U}
is
1_{M}
-safe.

Proof.

Clearly,
P\setminus H\subseteq P
remains a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay by Fact
4.6
. Moreover, it is immediate that
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{H}\setminus\{H\}
is an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
P\setminus H
such that every
G\in\mathbf{G}
is
1_{M}
-safe. Additionally, it is clear that
\alpha((P\setminus H)^{+})\subseteq\alpha(P^{+})
(our first induction parameter has not increased) and
\mathbf{G}\subsetneq\mathbf{H}
(our second parameter has decreased). Hence, we may apply induction in Lemma
5.15
for the case when
P,\mathbf{H}
and
s
have been replaced by
P\setminus H
,
\mathbf{G}
and
1_{M}
respectively. This yields an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{U}
of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
such that every
U\in\mathbf{U}
is
1_{M}
-safe.
∎

We fix the partition
\mathbf{U}
of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
given by Fact
5.17
for the remainder of the proof. We distinguish two independent subcases. Since
H
is an element of the partition
\mathbf{H}
of
P
, we have
H\subseteq P
. It is therefore immediate that the inclusion
\alpha(P^{*}H)\subseteq\alpha(P^{+})
holds. We use a different argument depending on whether this inclusion is strict or not.

Subcase 1: we have the equality
\alpha(P^{*}H)=\alpha(P^{+})
.
We handle this subcase with induction on our third parameter (
i.e.
, the size of
s\alpha(P^{*})
). Recall that we have to build an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
containing only
s
-safe languages.

Observe that the hypothesis that
H
is
1_{M}
-safe means that there exists some element
t\in M
satisfying
\alpha(H)=\{t\}
. Similarly, since every
U\in\mathbf{U}
is
1_{M}
-safe, there exists some element
r_{U}\in M
such that
\alpha(U)=\{r_{U}\}
. We fix these elements of
M
for the rest of this subcase. The construction of
\mathbf{K}
is based on the following lemma, which is where we use our hypotheses and induction.

Fact 5.18
.

For every
U\in\mathbf{U}
, there exists an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{W}_{U}
of
P^{*}
such that every
W\in\mathbf{W}_{U}
is
sr_{U}t
-safe.

Proof.

We fix
U\in\mathbf{U}
for the proof. Since
\mathbf{U}
is a partition of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
, we have
\alpha(U)\subseteq\alpha(P^{*})
which means that
r_{U}\in\alpha(P^{*})
. Thus, we have
sr_{U}t\in s\alpha(P^{*}H)
. Therefore,
sr_{U}t\alpha(P^{*})\subseteq s\alpha(P^{*}HP^{*})
and since
H\subseteq P
, we get
sr_{U}t\alpha(P^{*})\subseteq s\alpha(P^{+})
. Combined with our hypothesis in Subcase 1 (
i.e.
,
\alpha(P^{*}H)=\alpha(P^{+})
), this yields
sr_{U}t\alpha(P^{*})\subseteq s\alpha(P^{*}H)
. Finally, the hypothesis (
6
) of the inductive step yields the
strict
inclusion
sr_{U}t\alpha(P^{*})\subsetneq s\alpha(P^{*})
: the third parameter in our induction has decreased. On the other hand, the first two parameters have not increased, as they only depend on
P
and
\mathbf{H}
, which remain unchanged. Consequently, by induction, we may apply Lemma
5.15
in the case when
s\in M
has been replaced by
sr_{U}t\in M
. This yields the desired
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{W}_{U}
of
P^{*}
.
∎

We are ready to define the partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
. Using Fact
5.18
, we define,

\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{U}\cup\bigcup_{U\in\mathbf{U}}\{UHW\mid W\in\mathbf{W}_{U}\}.

It remains to show that
\mathbf{K}
is an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
P^{*}
and that every
K\in\mathbf{K}
is
s
-safe. Let us first verify that
\mathbf{K}
is a partition of
P^{*}
. Since
P
is a prefix code, every word
w\in P^{*}
admits a
unique
decomposition
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
with
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in P
. If no factor
w_{i}
belongs to
H
, then
w\in(P\setminus H)^{*}
and
w
belongs to some unique
U\in\mathbf{U}
. Otherwise, let
w_{i}
be the leftmost factor such that
w_{i}\in H
. This implies that
w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}\in(P\setminus H)^{*}
, which yields a unique
U\in\mathbf{U}
such that
w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}\in U
. Moreover,
w_{i+1}\cdots w_{n}\in P^{*}
, which yields a unique
W\in\mathbf{W}_{U}
such that
w_{i+1}\cdots w_{n}\in W
. It follows that
w\in UHW
, and that
UHW
is the unique element of
\mathbf{K}
containing
w
.

Let us next verify that every
K\in\mathbf{K}
belongs to
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
. If
K\in\mathbf{U}
, this is immediate by definition of
\mathbf{U}
in Fact
5.17
. Otherwise,
K=UHW
with
U\in\mathbf{U}
and
W\in\mathbf{W}_{U}
. We know that
U,H,W\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
: this an hypothesis for
H
and stated in Facts
5.17
and
5.18
for
U
and
W
. Furthermore, one may verify that the concatenation
UHW
is
unambiguous
since
P
is a prefix code,
U\subseteq(P\setminus H)^{*}
and
W\subseteq P^{*}
. Altogether, it follows that
K\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
.

Finally, we prove that every
K\in\mathbf{K}
is
s
-safe. If
K\in\mathbf{U}
, this is immediate since
K
is actually
1_{M}
-safe by definition of
\mathbf{U}
in Fact
5.17
. Otherwise,
K=UHW
with
U\in\mathbf{U}
and
W\in\mathbf{W}_{U}
. Consider
w,w^{\prime}\in K
. We show that
s\alpha(w)=s\alpha(w^{\prime})
. By definition,
\alpha(H)=\{t\}
and
\alpha(U)=\{r_{U}\}
which implies that
s\alpha(w)=str_{U}\alpha(x)
and
s\alpha(w^{\prime})=str_{U}\alpha(x^{\prime})
for
x,x^{\prime}\in W
. Moreover,
W\in\mathbf{W}_{U}
is
sr_{U}t
-safe by definition in Fact
5.18
. Therefore,
s\alpha(w)=s\alpha(w^{\prime})
. This concludes the proof of Subcase 1.

Subcase 2: we have the strict inclusion
\alpha(P^{*}H)\subsetneq\alpha(P^{+})
.
In this case, we conclude using induction on the first parameter (
i.e.
, the size of
\alpha(P^{+})
). Recall that our objective is to construct an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
containing only
s
-safe languages.

Consider a word
w\in P^{*}
. Since
P
is a prefix code,
w
admits a unique decomposition
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
with
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in P
. We may uniquely decompose
w
in two (possibly empty) parts: a prefix
w_{1}\cdots w_{i}\in((P\setminus H)^{*}H)^{*}
and a suffix in
w_{i+1}\cdots w_{n}\in(P\setminus H)^{*}
. Using induction, we construct
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partitions of the possible prefixes and suffixes. Then, we combine them to construct a partition of the whole set
P^{*}
. Actually, we already handled the suffixes: Fact
5.17
provides an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{U}
of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
. It remains to partition the prefixes. We do so this in the following fact, which is proved using the hypothesis of Subcase 2 and induction.

Fact 5.19
.

There exists an
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{V}
of
((P\setminus H)^{*}H)^{*}
such that every
V\in\mathbf{V}
is
1_{M}
-safe.

Proof.

Let
Q=(P\setminus H)^{*}H
. In view of Fact
4.7
,
Q
is a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay. We apply induction in Lemma
5.15
for the case when
P
has been replaced by
Q
. Doing so requires building an appropriate
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
Q
and proving that one of our induction parameters has decreased.

Let
\mathbf{F}=\{UH\mid U\in\mathbf{U}\}
. Since
\mathbf{U}
is a partition of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
and
P
is a prefix code, one may verify that
\mathbf{F}
is a partition of
Q=(P\setminus H)^{*}H
. Moreover, it only contains languages in
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
. Indeed, if
U\in\mathbf{U}
, then the concatenation
UH
is
unambiguous
since
U\subseteq(P\setminus H)^{*}
and
P
is a prefix code. Moreover,
U,H\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
by hypothesis. Finally,
UH
is
1_{M}
-safe since this is the case for both
U
and
H
by definition. It remains to show that our induction parameters have decreased. Since
Q=(P\setminus H)^{*}H
, it is clear that
Q^{+}\subseteq P^{*}H
. Now,
\alpha(P^{*}H)\subsetneq\alpha(P^{+})
by hypothesis in Subcase 2, whence
\alpha(Q^{+})\subsetneq\alpha(P^{+})
: our first induction parameter has decreased. Thus, we may apply Lemma
5.15
in the case when
P,\mathbf{H}
and
s
have been replaced by
Q
,
\mathbf{F}
and
1_{M}
respectively. This yields the desired
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{V}
of
((P\setminus H)^{*}H)^{*}
.
∎

We are ready to construct the
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
and conclude the main argument. Let
\mathbf{K}=\{VU\mid V\in\mathbf{V}\text{ and }U\in\mathbf{U}\}
. It is immediate by definition that
\mathbf{K}
is a partition of
P^{*}
since
P
is a prefix code and
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{U}
are partitions of
((P\setminus H)^{*}H)^{*}
and
(P\setminus H)^{*}
respectively (cf. the above discussion). Moreover, every
K\in\mathbf{K}
belongs to
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
. Indeed, one may verify that
K
is the
unambiguous
concatenation
VU
of
V\in\mathbf{V}
and
U\in\mathbf{U}
which both belong to
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
. It remains to prove that every
K\in\mathbf{K}
is
s
-safe. Let
w,w^{\prime}\in K
, we show that
s\alpha(w)=s\alpha(w^{\prime})
. By definition, we have
K=VU
with
V\in\mathbf{V}
and
U\in\mathbf{U}
. Therefore,
w=vu
and
w^{\prime}=v^{\prime}u^{\prime}
with
u,u^{\prime}\in U
and
v,v^{\prime}\in V
. Since
U
and
V
are both
1_{M}
-safe by definition, we have
\alpha(u)=\alpha(u^{\prime})
and
\alpha(v)=\alpha(v^{\prime})
. It follows that
s\alpha(w)=s\alpha(w^{\prime})
, which concludes the proof of Lemma
5.15
.
∎

6.
First logical characterization: first-order logic

We now turn to the logical characterizations of star-free closure. In this section, we present the first one. It generalizes a well-known theorem of McNaughton and Papert
McNaughton & Papert 1971
, which characterizes the star-free languages as those which can be defined by a sentence of first-order logic equipped with the linear ordering (
i.e.
,
\mathit{SF}=\mathit{SF}(\textup{ST})=\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<)$}
). Here, we extend this theorem to all classes
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
where
\mathscr{C}
is a prevariety. More precisely, we associate a set
\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}}
of first-order predicates to every prevariety
\mathscr{C}
(its definition is taken from
Place & Zeitoun 2019
). Then, we show that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
contains exactly the languages that can be defined by a sentence of first-order logic equipped with the predicates in
\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}}
(
i.e.
,
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
). First, we briefly recall the definition of first-order logic over words. Then, we present the theorem itself.

6.1.
Definitions

We view each word
w\in A^{*}
as a logical structure. Its domain is the set
\textup{{Pos}}(w)=\{0,\dots,|w|+1\}
of positions in
w
. A position
i
such that
1\leq i\leq|w|
carries a label in
A
. On the other hand,
0
and
|w|+1
are artificial
unlabeled
positions. We use first-order logic (
FO
) to express properties of words
w
: a formula can quantify over the positions of
w
with first-order variables and use a predetermined set of predicates to test properties of these positions. We also allow two constants “
\mathit{min}
” and “
\mathit{max}
” interpreted as the artificial unlabeled positions
0
and
|w|+1
. Let us briefly recall the definition.

Signatures.
A
signature
is a (possibly infinite) set of predicates interpreted over words in
A^{*}
. Consider a natural number
k\in\mathbb{N}
. A
predicate of arity
k
over
A
is defined by a symbol
P
and for every word
w\in A^{*}
, an interpretation of
P
as a relation of arity
k
over the set of positions of
w
. More precisely, with every word
w\in A^{*}
, the predicate
P
associates a set of
k
-tuples of positions of
w
(
i.e.
, a subset of
((\textup{{Pos}}(w))^{k}
). If
(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
is a
k
-tuple in this set, we shall say that
P(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
holds (in
w
)
. All predicates that we consider in practice are either unary (they have arity
1
) or binary (they have arity
2
). Let us present them.

First, we use
label predicates
. For every letter
a\in A
, we associate a unary predicate (also denoted by “
a
”). It is interpreted as the unary relation selecting all positions whose label is
a
: given a word
w\in A^{*}
and
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we have that
a(i)
holds when the label of
i
is “
a
”. In particular, if
a(i)
holds, then
i
cannot be one of the two artificial positions
0
and
|w|+1
. Abusing notation, we write “
A
” for the set of all label predicates. Moreover, we use a binary predicate “
<
”, interpreted as the linear ordering between positions. Given a word
w
and
i,j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we have that
{<}(i,j)
holds if
i<j
. For the sake of improved readability, we use the infix notation, writing
i<j
instead of
{<}(i,j)
.

Finally, with each class
\mathscr{C}
, we associate two
generic
sets of predicates. The first one, written
\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}}
, contains a binary “infix” predicate
I_{L}(x,y)
for every
L\in\mathscr{C}
. Given
w\in A^{*}
and two positions
i,j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we have
w\models I_{L}(i,j)
when
i<j

and

w(i,j)\in L
. The second set, written
\mathbb{P}_{\mathscr{C}}
, contains a unary “prefix” predicate
P_{L}(x)
for every
L\in\mathscr{C}
. Given
w\in A^{*}
and a position
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we have
w\models P_{L}(i)
when
0<i

and

w(0,i)\in L
.

Remark 6.1
.

All classes
\mathscr{C}
that we consider in practice are prevarieties. In particular, this implies that
A^{*}\in\mathscr{C}
. Hence, set
\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}}
always contains the linear order predicate, since “
x<y
” is clearly equivalent to “
I_{A^{*}}(x,y)
”. In particular, when
\mathscr{C}
is the trivial prevariety
\textup{ST}=\{\emptyset,A^{*}\}
, the signature
\mathbb{I}_{\textup{ST}}
contains only
I_{A^{*}}
and
I_{\emptyset}
. Since
I_{\emptyset}(i,j)

never holds
, using
\mathbb{I}_{\textup{ST}}
boils down to considering
\{<\}
.

First-order formulas.
With a signature
\mathbb{S}
(
i.e.
,
\mathbb{S}
is a possibly infinite set of predicates), we associate a set
\textup{FO}[\mathbb{S}]
of first-order formulas. They are built-up from simple expressions called
atomic formulas
. The atomic formulas can test properties of the positions that were quantified by first-order variables using either equality or the predicates in
\mathbb{S}
. More precisely, they are of the form:

x_{1}=x_{2}\quad\text{or}\quad P(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}),

where
P\in\mathbb{S}
is a predicate of arity
k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and, for every
i\leq k
,
x_{i}
is either a first-order variable or one of the two constants
min
and
max
(symbols can be repeated: it may happen that
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
are the same symbol for
i\neq j
). We define
\textup{FO}[\mathbb{S}]
as the least set of expressions containing the atomic formulas and closed under the following rules:

•

Disjunction
: if
\varphi
and
\psi
are
\textup{FO}[\mathbb{S}]
formulas, then so is
(\varphi\vee\psi)
.

•

Negation
: if
\varphi
is an
\textup{FO}[\mathbb{S}]
formula, then so is
(\neg\varphi)
.

•

Existential quantification
: for any first-order variable
x
, if
\varphi
is an
\textup{FO}[\mathbb{S}]
formula, then so is
(\exists x\ \varphi)
.

For the sake of improved readability, we omit the parentheses when there is no ambiguity. Moreover, we define the other standard logical connectives as abbreviations. We write
\varphi\wedge\psi
for
\neg((\neg\varphi)\vee(\neg\psi))
and
\varphi\Rightarrow\psi
for
(\neg\varphi)\vee\psi
. We also write
\forall x\ \varphi
for
\neg(\exists x\ \neg\varphi)
.

Finally, we use the standard notion of “
free variable
”. Let
\varphi
be an
\textup{FO}[\mathbb{S}]
formula. An occurrence of some variable
x
in
\varphi
is said to be
bound
when it occurs inside an atomic formula that is under the scope of a quantification
\exists x
. For example, in the following formula
a(x)\wedge\exists y\ (x<y\wedge b(y))\wedge\exists x\ c(x)
,
the occurrence of the variable
x
inside the atomic formula
c(x)
is bound. We say that a variable
x
is
free
in a formula
\varphi
if there
exists
an occurrence of
x
in
\varphi
that is
not
bound. For example, in the formula above, there also exist two occurrences of
x
that are not bound (inside the atomic formula
a(x)
and inside the atomic formula
x<y
). Hence,
x
is a free variable of this formula. A
sentence
is a formula that has no free variable.

Semantics.
We define when a word
w\in A^{*}
satisfies a
sentence

\varphi
(a fact that we denote by
w\models\varphi
). The definition is by structural induction on the sentence
\varphi
. This means that we actually need to give the semantics of
all formulas
, not just sentences. Indeed, in general, a sentence may have subformulas with free variables. To tackle this issue, we need the notion of variable assignment. Let
w\in A^{*}
be a word and let
\mathscr{X}
be a finite set of variables. We define an
assignment
of
\mathscr{X}
in
w
as a map
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
. Additionally, we canonically extend every such assignment as a map
\mu:\mathscr{X}\cup\{min,max\}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
by defining
\mu(min)=0
and
\mu(max)=|w|+1
.

Let
\varphi
be an
\textup{FO}[\mathbb{S}]
formula and let
\mathscr{X}
be a set of variables containing all free variables of
\varphi
. For every word
w\in A^{*}
and every assignment
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we write
w,\mu\models\varphi
when one the following properties hold:

•

\varphi:=
“
x_{1}=x_{2}
” and
\mu(x_{1})=\mu(x_{2})
holds.

•

\varphi:=
“
P(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
” for some predicate
P\in\mathbb{S}
and
P(\mu(x_{1}),\dots,\mu(x_{k}))
holds.

•

\varphi:=
“
\psi\vee\chi
” and either
w,\mu\models\psi
or
w,\mu\models\chi
.

•

\varphi:=
“
\neg\psi
” and
w,\mu\not\models\psi
(
w
does not satisfy
\psi
under
\mu
).

•

\varphi:=
“
\exists y\ \psi
” and there exists an assignment
\gamma:\mathscr{X}\cup\{y\}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such that
\mu(x)=\gamma(x)
for every
x\in\mathscr{X}\setminus\{y\}
and
w,\gamma\models\psi
.

The definition depends on an assignment
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
where
\mathscr{X}
contains
all
free variables of
\varphi
. In particular, it may happen that
\mathscr{X}
contains variables that are
not
free in
\varphi
. Yet, this is allowed only for the sake of simplifying the presentation: one may verify that whether
w,\mu\models\varphi
only depends on the restriction of
\mu
to the variables that are free in
\varphi
. In particular, when no variable is free variable (
i.e.
,
\varphi
is a sentence), whether
w,\mu\models\varphi
holds is independent from the assignment
\mu
. Hence, we simply write
w\models\varphi
in this case. Altogether, it follows that each sentence
\varphi
of
\textup{FO}[\mathbb{S}]
defines a language: we let
L(\varphi)=\{w\in A^{*}\mid w\models\varphi\}
.

Classes associated to first-order logic.
To every set of predicates
\mathbb{S}
, we associate a class of languages
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{S})
. It consists of all languages that can be defined by a sentence in
\textup{FO}[A,\mathbb{S}]
(that is, we use the signature
A\cup\mathbb{S}
, containing the label predicates
and
those in
\mathbb{S}
). For the sake of avoiding clutter, we often abuse terminology and speak of an
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{S})
-sentence to mean an
\textup{FO}[A,\mathbb{S}]
-sentence.

Example 6.2
.

Let
A=\{a,b\}
. Let us present some languages in
\textup{FO}(<)
. We use the following abbreviation in first-order sentences: we write “
x+1=y
” for the formula “
(x<y)\wedge\neg\exists z\ (x<z\wedge z<y)
”. In other words, “
+1
” is interpreted as the successor relation over positions. We have
A^{*}aA^{*}bA^{*}a\in\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<)$}
since it is defined by the following sentence:
\exists x\exists y\ (x<y)\wedge a(x)\wedge b(y))\wedge(\exists x\ a(x)\wedge(x+1=max))
. Moreover,
(ab)^{*}\in\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<)$}
as well, since it is defined by the following
\textup{FO}(<)
sentence:

\forall x\forall y\ \left(x+1=y\right)\Rightarrow\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}&\left(x=min\wedge y=max\right)\\
\vee&\left(x=min\wedge a(y)\right)\\
\vee&\left(a(x)\wedge b(y)\right)\\
\vee&\left(b(x)\wedge a(y)\right)\\
\vee&\left(b(x)\wedge y=max\right)\end{array}\right).

We are interested in classes
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
where
\mathscr{C}
is a prevariety. Indeed, we prove below that
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})=\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
in that case. However, when
\mathscr{C}
is a
group prevariety
, the presentation of
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
can be simplified in view of the following lemma.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a group prevariety. Then,
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{G}})=\textup{FO}(<,\mathbb{P}_{\mathscr{G}})
.

Proof.

The inclusion
\textup{FO}(<,\mathbb{P}_{\mathscr{G}})\subseteq\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{G}})
is immediate since all predicates in
\{<\}\cup\mathbb{P}_{\mathscr{G}}
can be simulated using those in
\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{G}}
. Indeed,
x<y
is equivalent to
I_{A^{*}}(x,y)
and
P_{L}(x)
(for
L\in\mathscr{G})
is equivalent to
I_{L}(min,x)
. We now prove that
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{G}})\subseteq\textup{FO}(<,\mathbb{P}_{\mathscr{G}})
. By definition, it suffices to prove that for every language
L\in\mathscr{G}
, the atomic formula
I_{L}(x,y)
is equivalent to a formula of
\textup{FO}(<,\mathbb{P}_{\mathscr{G}})
. Proposition
2.4
yields a
\mathscr{G}
-morphism
\alpha:A^{*}\to G
recognizing
L
. Since
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety, Lemma
3.5
implies that
G
is a group. Let
F\subseteq G
be the set such that
\alpha^{-1}(F)=L
.

For every
g\in G
, the language
\alpha^{-1}(g)
belongs to
\mathscr{G}
, whence
P_{\alpha^{-1}(g)}
is a predicate in
\mathbb{P}_{\mathscr{G}}
. Since
G
is a group, it is immediate that
\alpha(v)=(\alpha(ua))^{-1}\alpha(uav)
for all
u,v\in A^{*}
and
a\in A
. Therefore, one may verify that
I_{L}(x,y)
is equivalent to the following formula of
\textup{FO}(<,\mathbb{P}_{\mathscr{G}})
, where
T\subseteq G\times A\times G
is the set of all triples
(g,a,h)\in G\times A\times G
such that
(g\alpha(a))^{-1}h\in F
:

(x<y)\wedge\Big((x=min\wedge P_{L}(y))\vee\bigvee_{(g,a,h)\in T}\big(P_{\alpha^{-1}(g)}(x)\wedge a(x)\wedge P_{\alpha^{-1}(h)}(y)\big)\Big).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Example 6.4
.

Lemma
6.3
applies to important sets of predicates. First, if
\mathscr{G}
is the trivial prevariety
\textup{ST}=\{\emptyset,A^{*}\}
, all predicates in
\mathbb{P}_{\textup{ST}}
are trivial. Hence,
\textup{FO}(<,\mathbb{P}_{\textup{ST}})=\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<)$}
.

Next, let us consider the class
MOD
of
modulo languages
, consisting in Boolean combinations of languages
\{w\in A^{*}\mid|w|\equiv k\bmod m\}
with
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k<m
. In this case, we obtain first-order logic with
modular predicates
. For all
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k<m
, the set “
MOD
” of
modular predicates
contains a unary predicate
M_{k,m}
selecting the positions
i
such that
i\equiv k\bmod m
. One may use Lemma
6.3
to verify that
\textup{FO}(<,\mathbb{P}_{\textup{MOD}})=\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<,MOD)$}
.

Finally, we consider the class
AMT
of
alphabet modulo testable languages
. If
w\in A^{*}
and
a\in A
, we let
\#_{a}(w)\in\mathbb{N}
be the number of occurrences of letter
a
in
w
. The class
AMT
consists of all Boolean combinations of languages
\{w\in A^{*}\mid\#_{a}(w)\equiv k\bmod m\}
where
a\in A
and
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k<m
(these are the languages recognized by commutative groups). In this case, we obtain first-order logic with
alphabetic modular predicates
. For all
a\in A
and all
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
the set “
AMOD
” of
alphabetic modular predicates
contains a unary predicate
M^{a}_{k,m}
selecting the positions
i
such
\#_{a}(w(0,i))\equiv k\bmod m
). One may use Lemma
6.3
to verify that
\textup{FO}(<,\mathbb{P}_{\textup{AMT}})=\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<,AMOD)$}
.

6.2.
Main theorem

We may now present the main result of the section. It connects star-free closure to first-order logic for all input classes that are prevarieties.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a prevariety. Then,
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
.

Additionally, in view of Lemma
6.3
, Theorem
6.5
can be simplified when the input class is a group prevariety
\mathscr{G}
. More precisely, we have the following theorem.

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a group prevariety. Then,
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})=\textup{FO}(<,\mathbb{P}_{\mathscr{G}})
.

Remark 6.7
.

Corollary
6.6
has interesting applications when combined with Corollary
5.13
(
i.e.
, the algebraic characterization of the class
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
): we obtain that a regular language belongs to
\textup{FO}(<,\mathbb{P}_{\mathscr{G}})
if and only if the
\mathscr{G}
-kernel of its syntactic morphism is aperiodic. In particular when
\mathscr{G}=\textup{MOD}
, recall from Remark
5.8
that
MOD
-kernels correspond to a standard notion:
stable monoids
. Hence, we obtain that a regular language belongs to
\textup{FO}(<,MOD)
if and only if the stable monoid of its syntactic morphism is aperiodic. This is a well-known theorem of Barrington, Compton, Straubing and Thérien
Barrington et al. 1992
, whose original proof relies on entirely different techniques.

We now concentrate on the proof of Theorem
6.5
. Let us point out that both directions of the proofs are handled directly: we “translate”
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
sentences into expressions witnessing membership in
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
and vice-versa.

Proof of Theorem
6.5
.

We fix a prevariety
\mathscr{C}
and show that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
. The two inclusions are proved independently. We start with
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
, which is simpler.

Inclusion
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
.
By definition of
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
, it suffices to prove that
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
, that
\{a\}\in\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
for every
a\in A
and that
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
is closed under union, complement and concatenation. It is clear that every
L\in\mathscr{C}
is defined by the sentence
I_{L}(min,max)
of
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
. Therefore,
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
. Moreover, for every letter
a\in A
, the language
\{a\}
is defined by the sentence
\exists x\left(a(x)\wedge(min+1=x)\wedge(x+1=max)\right)
. It is also clear that
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
is closed under union and complement since Boolean connectives can be used freely in
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
sentences. It remains to prove that
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
is closed under concatenation. The argument is based on the following lemma.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
L\in\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
. There exists an
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
formula
\varphi_{L}(x,y)
with two free variables
x
and
y
such that for every
w\in A^{*}
and every positions
i,j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
in
w
such that
i<j
, we have
w\models\varphi_{L}(i,j)
if and only if
w(i,j)\in L

Proof.

By hypothesis, there exists a sentence
\psi
of
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
defining
L
. We build
\varphi_{L}(x,y)
from
\psi
by restricting quantifications with respect to the free variables
x,y
: we only allow quantification over positions between
x
and
y
. Moreover, we use
x
and
y
themselves as substitutes for the unlabeled positions. More precisely,
\varphi_{L}(x,y)
is defined by applying the following modifications to
\psi
:

(1)

Every subformula of the form
\exists z\ \Gamma
is recursively replaced by,

\exists z\ \left(\left((z=x)\vee(x<z\wedge z<y)\vee(z=y)\right)\wedge\Gamma\right).

(2)

All occurrences of the constant
min
are replaced by the free variable
x
and all occurrences of the constant
max
are replaced by
y
.

(3)

Every atomic subformula of the form
a(z)
for some
a\in A
is replaced by,

a(z)\wedge(x<z)\wedge(z<y).

One may verify that
\varphi_{L}(x,y)
satisfies the desired property.
∎

We may now prove that
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
is closed under concatenation. Let
K,L\in\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
. We prove that
KL\in\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
. Lemma
6.8
yields two formulas
\varphi_{K}(x,y)
and
\varphi_{L}(y,z)
such that for every
w\in A^{*}
and every positions
i,j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
in
w
such that
i<j
, we have
w\models\varphi_{K}(i,j)
if and only if
w(i,j)\in K
and
w\models\varphi_{L}(i,j)
if and only if
w(i,j)\in L
. It is now immediate that
KL
is defined by the following sentence of
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
:

\exists x\exists y\quad(x+1=y)\wedge\varphi_{K}(min,y)\wedge\varphi_{L}(x,max).

We obtain
KL\in\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
, as desired. This completes the proof of the first inclusion:
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
.

Inclusion
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})\subseteq\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
.
The proof of this inclusion is more involved. Yet, it is constructive as well: starting from an
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
sentence
\varphi
, we use structural induction on
\varphi
to prove that the language it defines may be built from basic languages in
\mathscr{C}
using Boolean combinations and concatenations. Of course, this means that we shall have to deal with formulas that are
not
sentences. We start with preliminary definitions.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. An
n
-scheme
is a tuple
\bar{L}=(L_{0},a_{1},L_{1},\dots,a_{n},L_{n})
where
L_{0},\dots,L_{n}\subseteq A^{*}
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
. Note that
0
-schemes are well-defined: they are simply languages. We write
\bar{L}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
to indicate that
L_{0},\dots,L_{n}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. Additionally, we define an
n
-blueprint
as a
finite
set
\mathbf{L}
of
n
-schemes. We write
\mathbf{L}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
to indicate that
\bar{L}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
for every
n
-scheme
\bar{L}\in\mathbf{L}
. Let us provide semantics for
n
-blueprints.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, an
n
-split
is a
linearly ordered
set
\mathscr{X}
of exactly
n+2
first-order variables. For the sake of avoiding clutter, we often make the linear ordering implicit. For example, if we say that the set
\mathscr{X}=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n+1}\}
is an
n
-split, we implicitly mean that the ordering is
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{n}<x_{n+1}
. Moreover, given an
n
-split
\mathscr{X}=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n+1}\}
and a word
w\in A^{*}
, we say that an assignment
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
is
correct
to indicate that,

0=\mu(x_{0})<\mu(x_{1})<\cdots<\mu(x_{n+1})=|w|+1.

Now, consider an
n
-blueprint
\mathbf{L}
and an
n
-split
\mathscr{X}=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n+1}\}
, a word
w\in A^{*}
and a correct assignment
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
. We say that
(w,\mu)
satisfies
\mathbf{L}
and write
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}
if and only if there exists an
n
-scheme
(L_{0},a_{1},L_{1},\dots,a_{n},L_{n})\in\mathbf{L}
which satisfies the two following conditions:

(1)

For all
1\leq i\leq n
, the position
\mu(x_{i})\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
is labeled by
a_{i}
.

(2)

For all
0\leq i\leq n
, we have
w(\mu(x_{i}),\mu(x_{i+1}))\in L_{i}
.

Observe that when
n=0
, there exists only one correct assignment
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
: we have
\mu(x_{0})=0
and
\mu(x_{1})=|w|+1
. Moreover, in that case,
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}
if and only there exists a language
L\in\mathbf{L}
such that
w\in L
.

The argument is based on the next proposition, proved by induction on the size of
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
formulas. The statement only applies to
constant-free
formulas (
i.e.
, which do not involve symbols “
min
” and “
max
”), a restriction we shall deal with when using it to complete the main proof.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathscr{X}
be an
n
-split and
\varphi
be a constant-free
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
-formula whose free variables are contained in
\mathscr{X}
. Then, there exists an
n
-blueprint
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
such that for all
w\in A^{*}
and for all correct assignment
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we have:

(7)

w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ w,\mu\models\varphi.

We first apply Proposition
6.9
to prove that
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})\subseteq\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. Let
L\in\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
. We prove that
L\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. The hypothesis
L\in\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
means that there is an
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
-sentence
\psi
defining
L
. Consider the
0
-split
\mathscr{X}=\{x_{0},x_{1}\}
. Since variables can be renamed, we may assume without loss of generality that
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
do
not
occur in
\psi
. Let
\varphi
be the formula obtained from
\psi
by replacing all occurrences of the constant symbols
min
and
max
by
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
, respectively. It follows from Proposition
6.9
that there exists a
0
-blueprint
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
satisfying (
7
). By definition
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
is a finite set of languages in
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. We let
H=\bigcup_{K\in\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}}K\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
and show that
H=L
to complete the proof. Consider a word
w\in A^{*}
and let
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
be the only correct assignment:
\mu(x_{0})=0
and
\mu(x_{1})=|w|+1
. By definition of
\varphi
from
\psi
, it is immediate that
w\in L\Leftrightarrow w,\mu\models\varphi
. It then follows from (
7
) that
w\in L\Leftrightarrow w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
. Finally by definition of
H
, we obtain
w\in L\Leftrightarrow w\in H
, as desired.

This concludes the main argument. It remains to prove Proposition
6.9
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathscr{X}
be an
n
-split and
\varphi
be a constant-free
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
formula whose free variables are contained in
\mathscr{X}
. We write
\mathscr{X}=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n+1}\}
. Moreover, we may assume without loss of generality that
\varphi
does not contain the equality predicate, as subformulas of the form “
y=z
” may be replaced by the equivalent formula “
\neg(y<z\vee z<y)
” (recall from Remark
6.1
that the linear ordering is available in
\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}}
). We use induction on the size of
\varphi
(
i.e.
, on the number of symbols in its syntax tree) to construct an
n
-blueprint
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
satisfying (
7
).

Atomic Formulas.
By hypothesis on
\varphi
there are only two kinds of atomic formulas: those involving the label predicates and those involving the predicates in
\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}}
. Note that since
\varphi
is constant-free, there are no atomic formulas involving the constants
min
and
max
.

Assume first that
\varphi:=
“
a(x_{h})
” for some
a\in A
and
h
such that
0\leq h\leq n+1
. There are two cases. If
h=0
or
h=n+1
, then it suffices to define
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}=\emptyset\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. Otherwise, we have
1\leq h\leq n
. We define
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
as the set of all
n
-schemes
(A^{*},a_{1},A^{*},\dots,a_{n},A^{*})
where
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
are letters such that
a_{h}=a
. it is clear that
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
and one may verify from the definitions that it satisfies (
7
).

Assume now that
\varphi:=
“
I_{L}(x_{g},x_{h})
” for
g,h
such that
0\leq g,h\leq n+1
and
L\in\mathscr{C}
. Recall that given a word
w\in A^{*}
and two positions
i,j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
,
I_{L}(i,j)
holds if and only if
i<j
and
w(i,j)\in L
. Hence, there are two cases. First, if
h\leq g
, it suffices to define
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}=\emptyset\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. Assume now that
g<h
. Since
\mathscr{C}
is a prevariety, Proposition
2.4
yields a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to N
recognizing
L
. Let
F\subseteq M
such that
L=\alpha^{-1}(F)
. We define a set of tuples
T\subseteq M\times(A\times M)^{n}
(when
n=0
, we have
T\subseteq M
) as follows:

T=\bigl\{(s_{0},a_{1},s_{1},\dots,a_{n},s_{n})\mid s_{g}\alpha(a_{g+1})s_{g+1}\cdots\alpha(a_{h-1})s_{h-1}\in F\bigr\}.

We define
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
as the set of all
n
-schemes of the form
(\eta^{-1}(s_{0}),\{a_{1}\},\eta^{-1}(s_{1}),\dots,\{a_{n}\},\eta^{-1}(s_{n}))
such that
(s_{0},a_{1},s_{1},\dots,a_{n},s_{n})\in T
. Since
\eta
is a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism, it is immediate that
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}\in\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. One may now verify from the definitions that
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
satisfies (
7
).

Disjunction.
Let us now assume that
\varphi:=
“
\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}
”. For
i=1,2
, induction yields an
n
-blueprint
\mathbf{L}_{i}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
which satisfies (
7
) for
\psi_{i}
. It is now immediate from the definitions that
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}=\mathbf{L}_{1}\cup\mathbf{L}_{2}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
satisfies (
7
) for
\varphi
.

Negation.
We assume that
\varphi:=
“
\neg\psi
”. Induction yields an
n
-blueprint
\mathbf{L}_{\psi}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
such that if
w\in A^{*}
and
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
is a correct assignment, then
w,\mu\in\mathbf{L}_{\psi}\Leftrightarrow w,\mu\models\psi
. Let
\bar{L}_{1},\dots,\bar{L}_{k}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
be the
n
-schemes such that
\mathbf{L}_{\psi}=\{\bar{L}_{1},\dots,\bar{L}_{k}\}
. Finally, for every
j\leq k
, we let
(L_{0,j},a_{1,j},L_{1,j},\dots,a_{n,j},L_{n,j})=\bar{L}_{j}
. Let
J\subseteq\{1,\dots,k\}
. We define,

H_{i,J}=A^{*}\setminus\Bigl(\bigcup_{j\in J}L_{i,j}\Bigr)\text{ for $0\leq i\leq n$}.

Note that
H_{i,\emptyset}=A^{*}
. Moreover, since
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
is closed under union and complement by definition, we have
H_{i,J}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. We now define
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
as the set of all
n
-schemes
(H_{0,J_{0}},b_{1},H_{1,J_{1}},\dots,b_{n},H_{n,J_{n}})
where
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in A
and
J_{0},\dots,J_{n}\subseteq\{1,\dots,k\}
are such that for every
j\leq k
, either
b_{i}\neq a_{i,j}
for some
i
such that
1\leq i\leq n
, or
j\in J_{i}
for some
i
such that
0\leq i\leq n
. By definition,
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
is an
n
-blueprint satisfying
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. Moreover, it is straightforward to verify that if
w\in A^{*}
and
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
is a correct assignment, then
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
if and only if
w,\mu\not\models\mathbf{L}_{\psi}
. Since
\varphi:=
“
\neg\psi
”, it is immediate that
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
satisfies (
7
), as desired.

First-order quantification.
Finally, assume that
\varphi:=
“
\exists y\ \psi
”. Since variables may be renamed, we may assume without loss of generality that
y\not\in\mathscr{X}=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n+1}\}
. We define
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
as the union of some
n
-blueprints that we build by induction. We use two kinds of
n
-blueprints in this union.

For every
i
such that
0\leq i\leq n+1
, let
\psi_{i}
be the formula obtained from
\psi
by replacing every free occurrence of the variable
y
with
x_{i}
. By definition all free variables in
\psi_{i}
belong to
\mathscr{X}
. Moreover, the size of
\psi_{i}
is the same as the one of
\psi
which is strictly smaller than the size of
\varphi:=
“
\exists y\ \psi
”. Consequently, induction yields an
n
-blueprint
\mathbf{L}_{i}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
such that if
w\in A^{*}
and
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
is a correct assignment, then
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{i}\Leftrightarrow w,\mu\models\psi_{i}
.

We turn to the second kind of
n
-blueprint. Let
i
such that
0\leq i\leq n
. We consider the linearly ordered set of first-order variables
\mathscr{Y}_{i}=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{i},y,x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n+1}\}
(
i.e.
,
\mathscr{Y}_{i}=\mathscr{X}\cup\{y\}
and
y
is placed between
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
for the linear ordering). Since the size of
\psi
is strictly smaller than the one of
\varphi:=
“
\exists y\ \psi
’, induction yields an
(n+1)
-blueprint
\mathbf{G}_{i}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
such that if
w\in A^{*}
and
\gamma:\mathscr{Y}_{i}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
is a correct assignment, then
w,\mu\models\mathbf{G}_{i}\Leftrightarrow w,\gamma\models\psi
. We use
\mathbf{G}_{i}
to define an
n
-blueprint
\mathbf{H}_{i}
. Let
\bar{G}=(G_{0},c_{1},G_{1},\dots,c_{n+1},G_{n+1})
be an arbitrary
(n+1)
-scheme. We associate to
\bar{G}
an
n
-scheme
f_{i}(\bar{G})=(H_{0},d_{1},H_{1},\dots,d_{n},H_{n})
as follows:

•

for every
j
such that
0\leq j\leq i-1
, we let
H_{j}=G_{j}
and
d_{j+1}=c_{j+1}
.

•

we let
H_{i}=G_{i}c_{i+1}G_{i+1}
.

•

for every
j
such that
i+1\leq j\leq n
, we let
d_{j}=c_{j+1}
and
H_{j}=G_{j+1}
.

Finally, we define
\mathbf{H}_{i}=\{f_{i}(\bar{G})\mid\bar{G}\in\mathbf{G}_{i}\}
. Observe that since
\mathbf{G}_{i}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
,
\{c_{i+1}\}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
and
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
is closed under concatenation, it is immediate that
\mathbf{H}_{i}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
.

We may now define the
n
-blueprint
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
. We let,

\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}=\Bigl(\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq n+1}\mathbf{L}_{i}\Bigr)\cup\Bigl(\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq n}\mathbf{H}_{i}\Bigr).

It it clear that
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
is an
n
-blueprint such that
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
since this is the case for all sets
\mathbf{L}_{i}
and
\mathbf{H}_{i}
by definition. Hence, it remains to verify that (
7
) is satisfied. Let
w\in A^{*}
and
\mu:\mathscr{X}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
a correct assignment. We prove that
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}\Leftrightarrow w,\mu\models\varphi
. There are two directions.

First, assume that
w,\mu\models\varphi
. We prove that
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
. Since
\varphi:=
“
\exists y\ \psi
”, there exists an assignment
\gamma:\mathscr{X}\cup\{y\}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such that
\gamma(x)=\mu(x)
for all
x\in\mathscr{X}
and
w,\gamma\models\psi
(note that at this stage,
\gamma
is not a “correct assignment”: indeed, this is not well-defined since we have not specified any linear ordering on
\mathscr{X}\cup\{y\}
) yet. We distinguish two cases. First, assume that there exists
i
satisfying
0\leq i\leq n+1
and
\gamma(y)=\gamma(x_{i})=\mu(x_{i})
. In that case, one may verify from the definitions that
w,\gamma\models\psi
entails
w,\mu\models\psi_{i}
. By definition of
\psi_{i}
, it follows that
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{i}
and therefore that
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
as desired, since
\mathbf{L}_{i}\subseteq\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
. In the second case, since
\mu(x_{0})=0
and
\mu(x_{n+1})=|w|+1
(by definition of increasing assignments), there exists
i
such that
0\leq i\leq n
and
\mu(x_{i})<\gamma(y)<\mu(x_{i+1})
. Hence,
\gamma
can be viewed as a
correct
assignment
\gamma:\mathscr{Y}_{i}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
. Since
w,\gamma\models\psi
, it follows that
w,\gamma\models\mathbf{G}_{i}
by definition of
\mathbf{G}_{i}
. One may then verify from the definition of
\mathbf{H}_{i}
from
\mathbf{G}_{i}
that
w,\mu\models\mathbf{H}_{i}
. Consequently,
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
again, since
\mathbf{H}_{i}\subseteq\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
by definition.

We turn to the converse implication. Assume that
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
. We prove that
w,\mu\models\varphi
. By definition of
\mathbf{L}_{\varphi}
as the union of smaller
n
-blueprints, there are two cases. In the first case, we assume that there exists
i
such that
0\leq i\leq n+1
and
w,\mu\models\mathbf{L}_{i}
. By definition of
\mathbf{L}_{i}
, it follows that
w,\mu\models\psi_{i}
. Moreover, by definition of
\psi_{i}
from
\psi
, this implies that
w,\gamma\models\exists y\ \psi
where
\gamma:\mathscr{X}\cup\{y\}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
is the assignment defined by
\gamma(x)=\mu(x)
for every
x\in\mathscr{X}
and
\gamma(y)=\mu(x_{i})
. Thus, since
\varphi:=
“
\exists y\ \psi
”, it follows that
w,\mu\models\varphi
, as desired. In the second case, we assume that there exists
i
such that
0\leq i\leq n
and
w,\mu\models\mathbf{H}_{i}
. By definition of
\mathbf{H}_{i}
from
\mathbf{G}_{i}
, one may verify that there exists a correct assignment
\gamma:\mathscr{Y}_{i}\to\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such
\mu(x)=\gamma(x)
for every
x\in\mathscr{X}
and
w,\gamma\models\mathbf{G}_{i}
. By definition of
\mathbf{G}_{i}
, it follows that
w,\gamma\models\psi
. Since
\varphi:=
“
\exists y\ \psi
”, it follows that
w,\mu\models\varphi
, concluding the proof.
∎

7.
Second logical characterization: linear temporal logic

We present a second logical characterization of star-free closure. It also generalizes a well-known result concerning the star-free languages: they are exactly those that can be defined in linear temporal logic (
LTL
). This is a consequence of Kamp’s theorem
Kamp 1968
which implies the equality
\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<)$}=\textup{LTL}
. It then follows from the theorem of McNaughton and Papert that
\mathit{SF}=\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<)$}=\textup{LTL}
. Here, we introduce a generalized definition of linear temporal logic, which is parameterized by a class
\mathscr{C}
. Actually, we define two classes
\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
and
\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})
. They generalize the two classical variants of linear temporal logic: without and with past, respectively. Then, we prove that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})
for every prevariety
\mathscr{C}
. The proof argument relies heavily on the characterizations of star-free closure that we already presented. The proof that
\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
is based on the equality
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
(Theorem
6.5
). Moreover, the proof that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
uses the algebraic characterization of star-free closure (Theorem
5.11
).

7.1.
Preliminaries

We first define the generalized notion of “linear temporal logic over finite words”. Then, we present some useful results about it, which we shall need later when proving the correspondence with star-free closure.

Syntax.
For every class
\mathscr{C}
, we define two sets of temporal formulas denoted by
\textup{LTL}[\mathscr{C}]
and
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
, which generalize the classical notions of “linear temporal logic” and “linear temporal logic with past”. We first define the
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
formulas, which are more general.

A particular formula is built from the atomic formulas using Boolean connectives and temporal modalities. The atomic formulas are:
min
,
max,\top
and
a
for every letter
a\in A
. Moreover, we allow all Boolean connectives: if
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
formulas, then so are
(\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2})
,
(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2})
and
(\neg\psi_{1})
. Finally, there are two binary temporal modalities “
until
” and “
since
”. They are parameterized by a language in
\mathscr{C}
. If
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
formulas and
L\in\mathscr{C}
, then the following expressions are
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
formulas as well:

(\psi_{1}\penalty\ \textup{U}_{L}\penalty\ \psi_{2})\quad\qquad\text{and}\quad\qquad(\psi_{1}\penalty\ \textup{S}_{L}\penalty\ \psi_{2}).

Moreover, we write “
U
” for “
\textup{U}_{A^{*}}
” and “
S
” for “
\textup{S}_{A^{*}}
” (their semantics will be the same as the standard modalities “
until
” and “
since
” in classical linear temporal logic). We also use the following abbreviations: given an
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
formula
\psi
and a language
L\in\mathscr{C}
, we write
\textup{F}_{L}\penalty\ \psi
for
\top\penalty\ \textup{U}_{L}\penalty\ \psi
and
\textup{X}\penalty\ \psi
for
(\neg\top)\penalty\ \textup{U}\penalty\ \psi
.

Finally, an
\textup{LTL}[\mathscr{C}]
formula is an
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
formula that only contains “
until
” modalities (
i.e.
, “
since
” is disallowed).

Semantics.
In order to evaluate an
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
formula
\varphi
, one needs a word
w\in A^{*}

and
a position
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
. We use structural induction to define when the pair
(w,i)
satisfies the formula
\varphi
. We denote this property by
w,i\models\varphi
:

•

Atomic formulas:
We always have
w,i\models\top
. For
a\in A
, we have
w,i\models a
when
a
is the letter at position
i
in
w
. Moreover, we have
w,i\models\mathit{min}
when
i=0
(
i.e.
,
i
is the leftmost unlabeled position) and
w,i\models\mathit{max}
when
i=|w|+1
(
i.e.
,
i
is the rightmost unlabeled position).

•

Disjunction:

w,i\models\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}
when
w,i\models\psi_{1}
or
w,i\models\psi_{2}
.

•

Conjunction:

w,i\models\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
when
w,i\models\psi_{1}
and
w,i\models\psi_{2}
.

•

Negation:

w,i\models\neg\psi
when
w,i\models\psi
does not hold.

•

Until:

w,i\models\psi_{1}\penalty\ \textup{U}_{L}\penalty\ \psi_{2}
when there exists
j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such that
i<j
,
w(i,j)\in L
, and,

(1)

For every
k\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such that
i<k<j
, we have
w,k\models\psi_{1}
, and,

(2)

w,j\models\psi_{2}
.

•

Since:

w,i\models\psi_{1}\penalty\ \textup{S}_{L}\penalty\ \psi_{2}
when there exists
j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such that
j<i
,
w(j,i)\in L
and,

(1)

For every
k\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such that
j<k<i
, we have
w,k\models\psi_{1}
, and,

(2)

w,j\models\psi_{2}
.

It remains to define what it means for a single word
w
(without any distinguished position) to satisfy an
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
formula
\varphi
: we evaluate formulas at the
leftmost unlabeled position
of each word. That is, we say that a word
w\in A^{*}
satisfies
\varphi
and write
w\models\varphi
if and only if
w,0\models\varphi
. The language
defined by the formula
\varphi
is
L(\varphi)=\{w\in A^{*}\mid w\models\varphi\}
.

Finally, we let
\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
(resp.
\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})
) be the class consisting of all languages defined by a formula in
\textup{LTL}[\mathscr{C}]
(resp.
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
). The classes
\textup{LTL}(\textup{ST})
and
\textup{LTLP}(\textup{ST})
(where
\textup{ST}=\{\emptyset,A^{*}\}
is the trivial prevariety) correspond to the classical variants of linear temporal logic from the literature. Typically, the classes
\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{G})
and
\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{G})
associated to some standard group prevariety
\mathscr{G}
(such as
MOD
or
AMT
), are also natural. We present an example using the class
MOD
of modulo languages.

Example 7.1
.

Let
A=\{a,b\}
. The language
(ab)^{*}
belongs to
\textup{LTL}(\textup{ST})
since it is defined by the
\textup{LTL}[\textup{ST}]
formula
\textup{X}\penalty\ (a\vee max)\wedge\bigl((a\Rightarrow\textup{X}\penalty\ b)\wedge(b\Rightarrow\textup{X}\penalty\ (a\vee max))\penalty\ \textup{U}\penalty\ max\bigr)
. Moreover
(aa+bb)^{*}\in\textup{LTL}(\textup{MOD})
. It is defined by the following
\textup{LTL}[\textup{MOD}]
formula:

(\textup{F}_{(AA)^{*}}\penalty\ max)\wedge\Bigl(\bigl((\textup{F}_{(AA)^{*}A}\penalty\ max)\Rightarrow((a\wedge\textup{X}\penalty\ a)\vee(b\wedge\textup{X}\penalty\ b))\bigr)\penalty\ \textup{U}\penalty\ max\Bigr).

Properties.
We present a few properties of the classes
\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
, which we shall use later for proving that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
(when
\mathscr{C}
is a prevariety). A key point is that the proof involves auxiliary arbitrary alphabets, independent from the alphabet
A
that we fix at the beginning. When
B
is such an alphabet, we need to specify what are the languages over
B
corresponding to the class
\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
(this is not clear since
\mathscr{C}
will be defined over our fixed alphabet
A
). We shall do so using morphisms
\eta:B^{*}\to N
that we obtain from the class
\mathscr{C}
.

Consider an alphabet
B
and a morphism
\eta:B^{*}\to N
into a finite monoid. An
\textup{LTL}[\eta]
formula is an
\textup{LTL}[\mathscr{D}]
formula
\varphi
where
\mathscr{D}
is the (finite) class consisting of all languages recognized by
\eta
(in particular, this means that atomic formulas in
\varphi
are
min
,
max
,
\top
and letters from
B
). Additionally, we write
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
for the class consisting of all languages (over
B
) that can be defined by an
\textup{LTL}[\eta]
formula. We complete these definitions with two lemmas, which are useful to build
\textup{LTL}[\eta]
formulas.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
B
be an alphabet,
\eta:B^{*}\to N
be a morphism into a finite monoid and
L\in\textup{LTL}(\eta)
. There exists a formula
\varphi\in\textup{LTL}[\eta]
such that for every
w\in B^{*}
and
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
we have
w,i\models\varphi
if and only if
w(i,|w|+1)\in L
.

Proof.

By definition, there exists a formula
\varphi\in\textup{LTL}[\eta]
such that
L(\varphi)=L
. Moreover, as
\varphi
is evaluated at the leftmost unlabeled position in words, we may assume without loss of generality that
\varphi
is a Boolean combination of formulas of the form
\psi_{1}\penalty\ \textup{U}_{H}\penalty\ \psi_{2}
. Since
\textup{LTL}[\eta]
formulas only contain “until” modalities by definition, it can now be verified that for every
w\in B^{*}
and
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
we have
w,i\models\varphi
if and only if
w(i,|w|+1)\in L
.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
B
be an alphabet,
\eta:B^{*}\to N
be a morphism into a finite monoid,
L\in\textup{LTL}(\eta)
and
\zeta\in\textup{LTL}[\eta]
. There exists a formula
\varphi\in\textup{LTL}[\eta]
such that for all
w\in B^{*}
and
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we have
w,i\models\varphi
if and only if there exists
j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
satisfying the three following conditions:

(1)

i<j
and for all
k\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such that
i<k<j
, we have
w,k\not\models\zeta
,

(2)

w,j\models\zeta
, and

(3)

w(i,j)\in L
.

Proof.

Lemma
7.2
yields a formula
\psi\in\textup{LTL}[\eta]
such that for every
w\in B^{*}
and
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
we have
w,i\models\psi
if and only if
w(i,|w|+1)\in L
. We build a new formula
\psi^{\prime}
by applying the two following modifications to
\psi
:

•

we replace every occurrence of the atomic formula
max
by
\zeta
.

•

we recursively replace every sub-formula of the form
\psi_{1}\penalty\ \textup{U}_{H}\penalty\ \psi_{2}
by
(\neg\zeta)\wedge((\psi_{1}\wedge\neg\zeta)\penalty\ \textup{U}_{H}\penalty\ \psi_{2})
.

It can now be verified that the formula
\varphi:=(\textup{F}\penalty\ \zeta)\wedge\psi^{\prime}
satisfies the property described in the lemma.
∎

7.2.
Main Theorem

It is well-known that we have
\mathit{SF}=\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<)$}=\textup{LTL}(\textup{ST})=\textup{LTLP}(\textup{ST})
. The equality
\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<)$}=\textup{LTL}(\textup{ST})=\textup{LTLP}(\textup{ST})
follows from Kamp’s theorem
Kamp 1968
(these equalities are only an instance of Kamp’s theorem, which is more general, as itconnects first-order logic to linear temporal logic for more general structures than finite words). Then, the equality
\mathit{SF}=\mbox{$\textup{FO}(<)$}
follows from the work of McNaughton and Papert
McNaughton & Papert 1971
. Here, we generalize this result to arbitrary input classes that are prevarieties. More precisely, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a prevariety. Then,
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})
.

Note that, in view of Corollary
5.12
, Theorem
7.4
implies that for every prevariety
\mathscr{C}
with decidable separation, the class
\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})
has decidable membership. We now concentrate on the proof of Theorem
7.4
.

Proof of Theorem
7.4
.

We fix a prevariety
\mathscr{C}
for the proof. The inclusion
\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})
is trivial. We prove that
\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
and
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
. Let us start with the former, which is simpler.

Inclusion

\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. The proof is based on Theorem
6.5
. It states the equality
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
. Thus, it suffices to prove that
\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
. Let
\varphi
be an
\textup{LTLP}[\mathscr{C}]
formula. We use structural induction on
\varphi
to build an
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
formula
[\varphi](x)
with at most one free variable
x
which satisfies the following condition:

(8)

\text{for all $w\in A^{*}$ and $i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)$,}\quad w,i\models\varphi\quad\text{if and only if}\quad w,x\mapsto i\models[\varphi](x),

where
x\mapsto i
denotes the assignment that maps
x
to
i
.
It will then be immediate that the language
L\subseteq A^{*}
defined by the
\textup{LTL}[\mathscr{C}]
formula
\varphi
(
i.e.
,
L=\{w\in A^{*}\mid w\models\varphi\}
) is defined by the
\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
sentence
[\varphi](min)
(which is obtained from
[\varphi](x)
by replacing every free occurrence of the variable
x
by the constant
min
). Hence, we obtain
\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
as desired.

If
\varphi=\top
, then we let
[\varphi](x):=(\mathit{min}=\mathit{min})
. If
\varphi=min
or
\varphi=max
, then we define
[\varphi](x):=(x=min)
or
[\varphi](x):=(x=max)
, respectively. If
\varphi=a
for some
a\in A
, then we let
[\varphi](x):=a(x)
. Logical connectives are handled in the usual way. It remains to treat the temporal operators
until
and
since
.

(1)

If
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\penalty\ \textup{U}_{L}\penalty\ \varphi_{2}
for some
L\in\mathscr{C}
, we define:

[\varphi](x):=\exists x_{2}\ I_{L}(x,x_{2})\wedge[\varphi_{2}](x_{2})\wedge\forall x_{1}\ (x<x_{1}\wedge x_{1}<x_{2})\Rightarrow[\varphi_{1}](x_{1}).

(2)

If
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\penalty\ \textup{S}_{L}\penalty\ \varphi_{2}
for some
L\in\mathscr{C}
, we define:

[\varphi](x):=\exists x_{2}\ I_{L}(x_{2},x)\wedge[\varphi_{2}](x_{2})\wedge\forall x_{1}\ (x_{2}<x_{1}\wedge x_{1}<x)\Rightarrow[\varphi_{1}](x_{1}).

It is simple to verify that this construction satisfies (
8
), as desired.

Inclusion

\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
. Let
L\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. We prove that
L\in\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
. The argument is based on Theorem
5.11
which implies that all the
\mathscr{C}
-orbits for the syntactic morphism
\alpha:A^{*}\to M
of
L
are aperiodic. We use this hypothesis and induction to construct an
\textup{LTL}[\mathscr{C}]
formula defining
L
. This implies that
L\in\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
, as desired.

The construction borrows ideas from the argument of Theorem
5.11
, which proves that under the same hypotheses on
L
, we have
L\in\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
. Yet, there are key differences, as
\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
and
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
are distinct formalisms. In particular, as mentioned above, we shall consider auxiliary alphabets independent from
A
. We start with preliminary definitions aimed at manipulating them.

First, each time we consider an auxiliary alphabet
B
, we shall have to recast the morphism
\alpha:A^{*}\to M
into a morphism
\beta:B^{*}\to M
and reformulate on
\beta
the hypothesis that all
\mathscr{C}
-orbits for
\alpha
are aperiodic. For this, we consider another morphism
\eta:B^{*}\to N
into a finite monoid. Roughly,
\eta
is used as an abstraction of the class
\mathscr{C}
over the alphabet
B
. We say that the pair
(\beta,\eta)
is
tame
to indicate that the following property holds:

(9)

For all
u,v\in B^{*}
, if
\eta(u)=\eta(v)
and
\beta(u)\in E(M)
, then
(\beta(uvu))^{\omega}=(\beta(uvu))^{\omega+1}
.

We first connect this definition to our hypothesis in the following simple fact.

Fact 7.5
.

There exists a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to N
such that the pair
(\alpha,\eta)
is tame.

Proof.

Lemma
5.3
yields a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to N
such that for every
u,v\in A^{*}
, if
\eta(u)=\eta(v)
, then
(\alpha(u),\alpha(v))
is a
\mathscr{C}
-pair. Since all
\mathscr{C}
-orbits for
\alpha
are aperiodic by hypothesis, it follows that if we additionally know that
\alpha(u)\in E(M)
, then
(\alpha(uvu))^{\omega}=(\alpha(uvu))^{\omega+1}
. Hence, (
9
) holds and
(\alpha,\eta)
is tame.
∎

Given an alphabet
B
, a morphism
\eta:B^{*}\to N
into a finite monoid and
P\subseteq B^{*}
, an
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition of
P
is a
finite
partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P
into languages of
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
. Moreover, given a morphism
\beta:B^{*}\to M
(where
M
is the original finite monoid used in
\alpha
) and
s\in M
, we say that
\mathbf{K}
is
(\eta,\beta,s)
-safe
to indicate that for every
K\in\mathbf{K}
and every
w,w^{\prime}\in K
, we have
\eta(w)=\eta(w^{\prime})
and
\beta(w)s=\beta(w^{\prime})s
. We may now start the proof. The argument is based on the following lemma.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
B
be an alphabet and consider a morphism
\beta:B^{*}\to M
into the fixed monoid
M
and another morphism
\eta:B^{*}\to N
into a finite monoid such that
(\beta,\eta)
is tame. Let
C\subseteq B
and
s\in M
. Then, there exists an
(\eta,\beta,s)
-safe
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition of
C^{*}
.

Let us first apply Lemma
7.6
to prove that
L\in\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
. Fact
7.5
yields a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to N
such that the pair
(\alpha,\eta)
is tame. Since
\eta
is a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism,
\textup{LTL}(\eta)\subseteq\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})
. Hence, it suffices to prove that
L\in\textup{LTL}(\eta)
. We apply the lemma for
B=C=A
,
\beta=\alpha
and
s=1_{M}
. This yields an
(\eta,\alpha,1_{M})
-safe
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
. Now,
\mathbf{K}
being
(\eta,\alpha,1_{M})
-safe implies that for every
K\in\mathbf{K}
, there exists
s\in M
such that
K\subseteq\alpha^{-1}(s)
. Since
\mathbf{K}
is a partition of
A^{*}
and
L
is recognized by
\alpha
, it follows that
L
is a union of languages of
\mathbf{K}
. Since
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
is closed under union, it follows that
L
itself belongs to
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
, which completes the main argument.

It remains to prove Lemma
7.6
. Let
B
be an alphabet and consider two morphisms
\beta:B^{*}\to M
and
\eta:B^{*}\to N
such that
(\beta,\eta)
is tame. Moreover, let
C\subseteq B
and
s\in M
. We build an
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition of
C^{*}
which is
(\eta,\beta,s)
-safe using induction on the three following parameters listed by order of importance:

(1)

The size of
\beta(C^{+})\subseteq M
.

(2)

The size of
C
.

(3)

The size of
\beta(C^{*})\cdot s\subseteq M
.

Remark 7.7
.

As already mentioned, the proof is similar to that of Theorem
5.11
. In particular, the current proof resembles to that of Corollary
5.13
. The reader may wonder why the element
s
, which serves as a buffer in these proofs, is not on the same side. The reason is that it was easier to consider
s\alpha(P^{*})
in Corollary
5.13
, due to the fact that
P
is a prefix code, while it is easier to consider
\beta(C^{*})\cdot s
here, due to the fact that we are dealing with pure future linear time temporal logic.

We distinguish two cases depending on the following property of
\beta
,
C
and
s
. We say that
s
is
(\beta,C)
-stable
when the following holds:

(10)

\text{for every $c\in C$,}\quad\beta(C^{*})\cdot s=\beta(cC^{*})\cdot s.

We first consider the case when
s
is
(\beta,C)
-stable. This is the base case. Otherwise, we use induction on our three parameters.

Base case:
s
is
(\beta,C)
-stable.
In that case, we define
\mathbf{K}=\{C^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)\mid t\in N\}
. Clearly, this is a finite partition of
C^{*}
. Moreover, it is clear that
C^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)\in\textup{LTL}(\eta)
for every
t\in N
. Indeed, it is defined by the
\textup{LTL}[\eta]
formula
\left(\left(\bigvee_{c\in C}c\right)\penalty\ \textup{U}_{\eta^{-1}(t)}\penalty\ max\right)
.

It remains to show that
\mathbf{K}
is
(\eta,\beta,s)
-safe. We use the hypothesis that
(\beta,\eta)
is tame. First, we use the hypothesis that
s
is
(\beta,C)
-stable to prove the following statement, analogous to Fact
5.16
.

Fact 7.8
.

Let
q,f\in\beta(C^{*})
such that
f
is idempotent. Then, we have
fqs=qs
.

Proof.

The proof is based on the following preliminary result. For
u,v\in C^{*}
, we show that,

(11)

there exists
r\in\beta(C^{*})
such that
\beta(u)rs=\beta(v)s
.

We fix
u,v\in C^{*}
for the proof of (
11
). We use induction on the length of
u
. If
u=\varepsilon
, it suffices to choose
r=\beta(v)\in\beta(C^{*})
. Otherwise,
u=u^{\prime}c
with
u^{\prime}\in C^{*}
,
c\in C
. Induction yields
r^{\prime}\in\beta(C^{*})
such that
\beta(u^{\prime})r^{\prime}s=\beta(v)s
. Moreover, since
s
is
(\beta,C)
-stable and
r^{\prime}\in\beta(C^{*})
, it follows from (
10
) that there exists
r\in\beta(C^{*})
such that
r^{\prime}s=\beta(c)rs
. Altogether, this yields
\beta(u^{\prime})\beta(c)rs=\beta(v)s
and as
u=u^{\prime}c
, we get
\beta(u)rs=\beta(v)s
, concluding the proof of (
11
).

We now prove the fact. Let
q,f\in\beta(C^{*})
such that
f
is idempotent. By definition, we
u,v\in C^{*}
such that
q=\beta(v)
and
f=\beta(u)
. Hence, (
11
) yields
r\in\alpha(C^{*})
such that
frs=qs
. Since
f
is idempotent, this implies that
fqs=ffrs=frs=qs
.
∎

We now prove that every
K\in\mathbf{K}
is
(\eta,\beta,s)
-safe. By definition,
K=C^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)
for
t\in N
. Given
u,v\in K
