, we have to show that
\eta(u)=\eta(v)
and
\beta(u)s=\beta(v)s
. Let
n=\omega(M)
. Since
u,v\in K
, we have
\eta(u)=\eta(v)=t
. Hence,
\eta(u^{n})=\eta(u^{n-1}v)
and since
\beta(u^{n})
is idempotent, the hypothesis that
(\beta,\eta)
is tame yields
(\beta(u^{2n-1}vu^{n}))^{n}=(\beta(u^{2n-1}vu^{n}))^{n+1}
. We now multiply by
s
on the right to obtain
(\beta(u^{2n-1}vu^{n}))^{n}s=(\beta(u^{2n-1}vu^{n}))^{n+1}s
. Since
n=\omega(M)
, we know that
(\beta(u^{2n-1}vu^{n}))^{n}
is an idempotent of
\beta(C^{*})
. Therefore, Fact
7.8
yields
(\beta(u^{2n-1}vu^{n}))^{n}s=s
. Altogether, we obtain that
s=\beta(u^{2n-1}vu^{n})s
. We now multiply by
\beta(u)
on the left to get
\beta(u)s=\beta(u^{2n}vu^{n})s
. Finally,
\beta(u^{n})\in\beta(C^{*})
is an idempotent, we may apply Fact
7.8
twice to get
\beta(u^{2n}vu^{n})s=\beta(v)s
. Altogether, this yields
\beta(u)s=\beta(v)s
, as desired.

Inductive case:
s
is not
(\beta,C)
-stable.
By hypothesis, there exists some letter
c\in C
such that the following property holds:

(12)

\beta(cC^{*})\cdot s\subsetneq\beta(C^{*})\cdot s.

We fix this letter
c\in C
for the rest of the argument and we let
D
be the sub-alphabet
D=C\setminus\{c\}
.

The restrictions
\beta:D^{*}\to M
and
\eta:D^{*}\to N
still form a tame pair
(\beta,\eta)
. Therefore, we may apply induction in Lemma
7.6
when replacing
C
by
D
.
Indeed, the first induction parameter (the size of
\beta(C^{+})
) has not increased here since
D\subseteq C
and
\beta
remains unchanged, while the second parameter has decreased:
|D|<|C|
. This yields an
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition
\mathbf{H}
of
D^{*}
. We may assume without loss of generality that
H\neq\emptyset
for every
H\in\mathbf{H}
.

Fact 7.9
.

There exists an
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition
\mathbf{H}
of
D^{*}
made of nonempty languages.

We distinguish two independent subcases. Observe that the inclusion
\beta(cC^{*})\subseteq\beta(C^{+})
holds. The argument differs depending on whether it is strict or not.

Subcase 1:
\beta(cC^{*})=\beta(C^{+})
.
We use induction on our third parameter (
i.e.
, the size of
\beta(C^{*})s
). Let
H\in\mathbf{H}
. Since
\mathbf{H}
is a partition of
D^{*}
which is
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe by definition and
H\neq\emptyset
, there exists a unique element
t_{H}\in\beta(D^{*})
such that
\beta(x)=t_{H}
for every
x\in H
. The construction of
\mathbf{K}
is based on the following fact (this is where we use induction).

Fact 7.10
.

For all
H\in\mathbf{H}
, there exists an
(\eta,\beta(c)t_{H}s)
-safe
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition
\mathbf{U}_{H}
of
C^{*}
.

Proof.

We fix
H\in\mathbf{H}
. Since
t_{H}\in\beta(D^{*})
, we have
\beta(c)t_{H}s\in\beta(cD^{*})s
. Therefore, we have
\beta(C^{*})\beta(c)t_{H}s\subseteq\beta(C^{+})s
. Combined with our hypothesis in Subcase 1 (
i.e.
,
\beta(cC^{*})=\beta(C^{+})
), this yields
\beta(C^{*})\beta(c)t_{H}s\subseteq\beta(cC^{*})s
. Finally, we obtain from (
6
) (
i.e.
,
\beta(cC^{*})s\subsetneq\beta(C^{*})s
) that the
strict
inclusion
\beta(C^{*})\beta(c)t_{H}s\subsetneq\beta(C^{*})s
holds. Hence, we may apply induction on our third parameter in Lemma
7.6
(
i.e.
, the size of
\beta(C^{*})s
) to obtain the desired finite partition
\mathbf{U}_{H}
of
C^{*}
which is
(\eta,\beta(c)t_{H}s)
-safe. Note that here, our first two parameters have not increased as
\beta
and
C
remain unchanged.
∎

We may now define the desired partition
\mathbf{K}
of
C^{*}
. Using the partitions
\mathbf{U}_{H}
given by Fact
7.10
, we define,

\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{H}\cup\{UcH\mid H\in\mathbf{H}\text{ and }U\in\mathbf{U}_{H}\}.

It remains to show that
\mathbf{K}
is indeed an
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition of
C^{*}
which is
(\eta,\beta,s)
-safe. One may verify that
\mathbf{K}
is a partition of
C^{*}
since
\mathbf{H}
is a partition of
D^{*}
and
\mathbf{U}_{H}
is a partition of
C^{*}
for every
H\in\mathbf{H}
(recall that
D=C\setminus\{c\}
). Let us prove that every
K\in\mathbf{K}
belongs to
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
. This is immediate if
K\in\mathbf{H}
by hypothesis on
\mathbf{H}
. Otherwise, there exist
H\in\mathbf{H}
and
U\in\mathbf{U}_{H}
such that
K=UcH
. Since
H\in\textup{LTL}(\eta)
, it follows from Lemma
7.2
that there exists a formula
\psi_{H}\in\textup{LTL}[\eta]
such that for every
w\in B^{*}
and every
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we have
w,i\models\psi_{H}
if and only if
w(i,|w|+1)\in H
. Moreover, let
\zeta\in\textup{LTL}[\eta]
be the formula
\zeta:=c\wedge\neg\textup{F}\penalty\ c
(given a word
w\in B^{*}
and
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we have
w,i\models\zeta
if and only if
i
is the rightmost position in
w
carrying the letter
c
). Since
U\in\textup{LTL}(\eta)
(by definition of
\mathbf{U}_{H}
), Lemma
7.3
yields a formula
\psi_{U}\in\textup{LTL}[\eta]
such that for every
w\in B^{*}
, we have
w\models\psi_{U}
if and only if there exists
j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such that
w,j\models\zeta
(by definition of
\zeta
,
j
must be unique) and
w(0,j)\in U
. Since
c\not\in D
and
H\subseteq D^{*}
, one may now verify that
K=UcH
is defined by the formula
\psi_{U}\wedge\textup{F}\penalty\ (\zeta\wedge\psi_{H})
. Hence, we get
K\in\textup{LTL}(\eta)
, as desired.

It remains to prove that
\mathbf{K}
is
(\eta,\beta,s)
-safe. Consider
K\in\mathbf{K}
and
w,w^{\prime}\in K
. We have to show that
\eta(w)=\eta(w^{\prime})
and
\beta(w)s=\beta(w^{\prime})s
. By definition of
\mathbf{K}
, there are two cases: first, if
K\in\mathbf{H}
we know by Fact
7.9
that
\mathbf{H}
is an
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition of
D^{*}
. Therefore, we obtain
\eta(w)=\eta(w^{\prime})
and
\beta(w)=\beta(w^{\prime})
, whence
\beta(w)s=\beta(w^{\prime})s
, as desired. Otherwise,
K=UcH
with
H\in\mathbf{H}
and
U\in\mathbf{U}_{H}
. Thus, we get
x,x^{\prime}\in H
and
u,u^{\prime}\in U
such that
w=ucx
and
w^{\prime}=u^{\prime}cx^{\prime}
. By definition of
t_{H}
, we have
\beta(x)=\beta(x^{\prime})=t_{H}
. Moreover, since
\mathbf{H}
is
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe and
\mathbf{U}_{H}
is
(\eta,\beta(c)t_{H}s)
-safe by Fact
7.10
, we also have
\eta(x)=\eta(x^{\prime})
and
\eta(u)=\eta(u^{\prime})
, whence
\eta(w)=\eta(w^{\prime})
. Finally,
\beta(w)s=\beta(u)\beta(c)t_{H}s
and
\beta(w^{\prime})s=\beta(u^{\prime})\beta(c)t_{H}s
and since
\mathbf{U}_{H}
is
(\eta,\beta(c)t_{H}s)
-safe, we obtain
\beta(w)s=\beta(w^{\prime})s
. This concludes the proof of this subcase.

Subcase 2:
\beta(cC^{*})\subsetneq\beta(C^{+})
.
We use induction on our first parameter (
i.e.
, the size of
\beta(C^{+})
). Consider a word
w\in C^{*}
. Since
D=C\setminus\{c\}
,
w
admits a unique decomposition
w=uv
such that
u\in D^{*}
and
v\in(cD^{*})^{*}
(
i.e.
,
u
is the largest prefix of
w
in
D^{*}
and
v
is the corresponding suffix). Using induction, we construct
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partitions of the possible prefixes and suffixes. Then, we combine them to construct a partition of the whole set
C^{*}
. Actually, not that we already partitioned the set of prefixes: we have an
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition
\mathbf{H}
of
D^{*}
which is
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe. It remains to partition the set of suffixes: this is where we use induction.

Lemma 7.11
.

There exists an
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition
\mathbf{V}
of
(cD^{*})^{*}
which is
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe.

Proof.

For each language
H\in\mathbf{H}
, we create a letter written
\mathbb{b}_{H}
and let
\mathbb{B}=\{\mathbb{b}_{H}\mid H\in\mathbf{H}\}
as a new alphabet. Moreover, we define new morphisms
\gamma:\mathbb{B}^{*}\to M
and
\delta:\mathbb{B}^{*}\to N
. Let
H\in\mathbf{H}
and consider the letter
\mathbb{b}_{H}
. Since
\mathbf{H}
is a partition of
D^{*}
which is
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe by definition and
H\neq\emptyset
, there exist unique elements
t_{H}\in\beta(D^{*})
and
q_{H}\in\eta(D^{*})
such that
\eta(x)=q_{H}
and
\beta(x)=t_{H}
for every
x\in H
. We let
\gamma(\mathbb{b}_{H})=\beta(c)t_{H}
and
\delta(\mathbb{b}_{H})=\eta(c)q_{H}
.

Observe that the pair
(\gamma,\delta)
is tame. Indeed, let
u,v\in\mathbb{B}^{*}
such that
\delta(u)=\delta(v)
and
\gamma(u)\in E(M)
. By definition of
\mathbb{B}
, there exist
w_{u},w_{v}\in(cD^{*})^{*}
such that
\gamma(u)=\beta(w_{u})
,
\gamma(v)=\beta(w_{v})
,
\delta(u)=\eta(w_{u})
and
\delta(v)=\eta(w_{v})
. Hence,
\eta(w_{u})=\eta(w_{v})
and
\beta(w_{u})\in E(M)
. Since
(\beta,\eta)
is tame, it then follows from (
9
) that
(\beta(w_{u}w_{v}w_{u}))^{\omega}=(\beta(w_{u}w_{v}w_{u}))^{\omega+1}
. This exactly says that
(\gamma(uvu))^{\omega}=(\gamma(uvu))^{\omega+1}
, as desired. Moreover, by definition of
\mathbb{B}
, one may verify that
\gamma(\mathbb{B}^{+})=\beta((cD)^{+})\subseteq\beta(cC^{*})
. Hence, since
\beta(cC^{*})\subsetneq\beta(C^{+})
(this is our hypothesis in Subcase 2), we get
|\gamma(\mathbb{B}^{+})|<|\beta(C^{+})
. Consequently, we may apply induction on the first parameter in Lemma
7.6
(
i.e.
, the size of
\beta(C^{+})
) to get an
\textup{LTL}(\delta)
-partition
\mathbf{G}
of
\mathbb{B}^{*}
which is
(\delta,\gamma,1_{M})
-safe. We use it to construct
\mathbf{V}
.

First, we define a map
\mu:(cD^{*})^{*}\to\mathbb{B}^{*}
. Observe that since
c\not\in D
, every word
u\in(cD^{*})^{*}
admits a unique decomposition
u=cu_{1}\cdots cu_{n}
with
u_{1},\dots u_{n}\in D^{*}
. For every
i\leq n
, we let
H_{i}
as the unique language in
\mathbf{H}
such that
u_{i}\in H_{i}
(recall that
\mathbf{H}
is partition of
D^{*}
). We then define
\mu(w)=\mathbb{b}_{H_{1}}\cdots\mathbb{b}_{H_{n}}
. Note that by definition, each position
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
which is labeled by a “
c
” corresponds to a unique position in
\mu(w)
. We may now define
\mathbf{V}=\{\mu^{-1}(G)\mid G\in\mathbf{G}\}
. It remains to show that
\mathbf{V}
is an
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition of
(cD^{*})^{*}
which is
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe. Clearly,
\mathbf{V}
is a partition of
(cD^{*})^{*}
by definition since
\mathbf{G}
is a partition of
\mathbb{B}^{*}
.

We first prove that every
V\in\mathbf{V}
belongs to
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
. By definition,
V=\mu^{-1}(G)
for some
G\in\mathbf{G}
. Let
\zeta:=c\vee max\in\textup{LTL}[\eta]
. We know that every
H\in\mathbf{H}
belongs to
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
. Hence, Lemma
7.3
yields a formula
\psi^{\prime}_{H}\in\textup{LTL}[\eta]
such that for every
w\in B^{*}
and
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we have
w,i\models\psi^{\prime}_{H}
if and only if there exists
j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such that
i<j
,
w,j\models\zeta
,
w,k\not\models\zeta
for every
i<k<j
and
w(i,j)\in H
. We let
\psi_{H}=c\wedge\psi^{\prime}_{H}
. By definition,
w,i\models\psi^{\prime}_{H}
if and only if
i
has label
c
and the greatest prefix of
w(i,|w|+1)
which is in
D^{*}
belongs to
H
. The key idea is that when
w\in(cD^{*})^{*}
, the formula
\psi_{H}
holds for the positions
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
which are labeled by
c
and such that the position of
\mu(w)\in\mathbb{B}^{*}
corresponding to
i
is labeled by
\mathbb{b}_{H}\in\mathbb{B}
. Moreover, since
G\in\mathbf{G}
, there exists an
\textup{LTL}[\delta]
formula
\Gamma_{G}
defining
G
by hypothesis on
\mathbf{G}
. We modify
\Gamma_{G}
into an
\textup{LTL}[\eta]
formula
\varphi_{V}
defining
V=\mu^{-1}(G)
. First, let
\varphi^{\prime}_{V}
be the formula obtained from
\Gamma_{G}
by applying the two following modifications:

(1)

We replace each atomic sub-formula “
\mathbb{b}_{H}
” for
H\in\mathbf{H}
by the
\textup{LTL}[\eta]
formula
\psi_{H}
.

(2)

We recursively replace all sub-formulas
\varphi_{1}\penalty\ \textup{U}_{X}\penalty\ \varphi_{2}
. Since
\Gamma_{G}
is an
\textup{LTL}[\delta]
-formula, we have
F\subseteq N
such that
X=\delta^{-1}(F)
. We recursively replace
\varphi_{1}\penalty\ \textup{U}_{X}\penalty\ \varphi_{2}
by,

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\left(\textup{X}\penalty\ \zeta\wedge(\zeta\Rightarrow\varphi_{1})\penalty\ \textup{U}_{\eta^{-1}(F)}\penalty\ (\zeta\wedge\varphi_{2})\right)\\
\vee&\left(\left(\neg\textup{X}\penalty\ \zeta\right)\wedge\left(\left(\neg\textup{X}\penalty\ \zeta\right)\penalty\ \textup{U}\penalty\ \left(\textup{X}\penalty\ \zeta\wedge\left((\zeta\Rightarrow\varphi_{1})\penalty\ \textup{U}_{\eta^{-1}(F)}\penalty\ (\zeta\wedge\varphi_{2})\right)\right)\right)\right).\end{array}

Finally, we let
\varphi_{V}:=\textup{X}\penalty\ (c\vee max)\wedge\varphi^{\prime}_{V}
. One may now verify from the definition that
\varphi_{V}
defines
V=\mu^{-1}(G)
.

It remains to prove that
\mathbf{V}
is
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe. Let
V\in\mathbf{V}
and
v,v^{\prime}\in V
. By definition, there exists
G\in\mathbf{G}
such that
V=\mu^{-1}(G)
. Hence, we have
\mu(v),\mu(v^{\prime})\in G
and since
\mathbf{G}
is
(\delta,\gamma,1_{M})
-safe, this yields
\gamma(\mu(v))=\gamma(\mu(v^{\prime}))
and
\delta(\mu(v))=\delta(\mu(v^{\prime}))
. One may verify that
\delta(\mu(v))=\eta(v)
,
\delta(\mu(v^{\prime}))=\eta(v^{\prime})
,
\gamma(\mu(v))=\beta(v)
and
\gamma(\mu(v^{\prime}))=\beta(v^{\prime})
. Thus, we get
\beta(v)=\beta(v^{\prime})
and
\delta(v)=\delta(v^{\prime})
, concluding the proof.
∎

We are ready to build our
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
C^{*}
. Let
\mathbf{V}
be the
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
-partition of
(cD^{*})^{*}
given by Lemma
7.11
. We let,

\mathbf{K}=\{HV\mid H\in\mathbf{H}\text{ and }V\in\mathbf{V}\}.

It is immediate by definition that
\mathbf{K}
is a partition of
C^{*}
since
D=C\setminus\{c\}
and
\mathbf{H}
is a partition of
D^{*}
. Let us verify that every
K\in\mathbf{K}
belongs to
\textup{LTL}(\eta)
. By definition, one can write
K=HV
for some
H\in\mathbf{H}
and
V\in\mathbf{V}
. Let
\zeta:=c\vee max
. Since
H\in\textup{LTL}(\eta)
, Lemma
7.3
yields an
\textup{LTL}[\eta]
formula
\psi_{H}
such that for every
w\in B^{*}
, we have
w\models\psi_{H}
if and only if there exists
j\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)\setminus\{0\}
such that
w,j\models\zeta
,
w,k\not\models\zeta
for every
k\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
such that
0<k<j
and
w(0,j)\in H
. Since
V\in\textup{LTL}(\eta)
(by hypothesis on
\mathbf{V}
), Lemma
7.2
yields an
\textup{LTL}[\eta]
formula
\psi_{V}
such that for every
w\in B^{*}
and every
i\in\textup{{Pos}}(w)
, we have
w,i\models\psi_{V}
if and only if
w(i,|w|+1)\in V
. One may now verify that
K=HV
is defined by the formula,

\psi_{H}\wedge\left(\left(\textup{X}\penalty\ \zeta\wedge\psi_{V}\right)\vee\left(\left(\neg\textup{X}\penalty\ \zeta\right)\wedge\left(\left(\neg\textup{X}\penalty\ \zeta\right)\penalty\ \textup{U}\penalty\ \left(\textup{X}\penalty\ \zeta\wedge\psi_{V}\right)\right)\right)\right).

Hence, we get
K\in\textup{LTL}(\eta)
. It remains to verify that
\mathbf{K}
is
(\eta,\beta,s)
-safe (it is in fact
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe). Let
K\in\mathbf{K}
and
w,w^{\prime}\in K
, we show that
\eta(w)=\eta(w^{\prime})
and
\beta(w)=\beta(w^{\prime})
(which implies
\beta(w)s=\beta(w^{\prime})s
). By definition,
K=HV
with
H\in\mathbf{H}
and
V\in\mathbf{V}
. Therefore,
w=uv
and
w^{\prime}=u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
with
u,u^{\prime}\in H
and
v,v^{\prime}\in V
. Since
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{V}
are both
(\eta,\beta,1_{M})
-safe by definition, we have
\eta(u)=\eta(u^{\prime})
,
\beta(u)=\beta(u^{\prime})
,
\eta(v)=\eta(v^{\prime})
and
\beta(v)=\beta(v^{\prime})
. It follows that
\eta(w)=\eta(w^{\prime})
and
\beta(w)=\beta(w^{\prime})
which concludes the proof.
∎

8.
Rating maps

We now turn to separation and covering. We prove two results in the paper. In Section
9
, we show that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-covering is decidable for every
finite
prevariety
\mathscr{C}
. Then, in Section
10
, we prove that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
-covering is decidable for every group prevariety
\mathscr{G}
that has decidable separation. In both cases, the algorithms are based on a generic framework which was introduced in
Place & Zeitoun 2018
for the specific purpose of handling separation and covering. It relies on simple algebraic objects called
rating maps
. We recall this framework in this preliminary section.

We define rating maps and present two particular kinds: the nice and the multiplicative ones. We use this notion to associate a computational problem with each lattice
\mathscr{C}
: “given as input a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho
and a regular language
L
, compute an optimal
\mathscr{C}
-cover of
L
for
\rho
”. Then, we connect this problem to
\mathscr{C}
-covering. Finally, we present new notions that are not defined in
Place & Zeitoun 2018
. They are specifically designed for handling the classes of the form
Op(\mathscr{C})
built from an input class
\mathscr{C}
using an operator. In the paper, we are interested in the case when
Op
is star-free closure.

8.1.
Definition

We first introduce
rating algebras
. A
rating algebra
is a monoid
(R,+)
which is
commutative
(
q+r=r+q
for every
q,r\in R
) and
idempotent
(
r+r=r
for all
r\in R
). The binary operation
+
is called
addition
and we denote the neutral element of
R
by
0_{R}
(we use an additive notation here, since we are dealing with a commutative monoid). Given a rating algebra
R
, we also define a canonical ordering “
\leq
” over
R
as follows:

\text{for all }r,s\in R,\quad r\leq s\text{ when }r+s=s.

One may verify that
\leq
is a partial order and that it makes
R
an ordered monoid (
i.e.
,
\leq
is compatible with addition). It can be verified that every morphism between two rating algebras is
increasing
for the canonical orderings. We often use this property implicitly.

Example 8.1
.

For every set
S
, the algebra
(2^{S},\cup)
is a rating algebra whose neutral element is
\emptyset
. The canonical ordering “
\leq
” on
2^{S}
is inclusion. Indeed, given
P,Q\in 2^{S}
, it is clear that
P\subseteq Q
if and only if
P\cup Q=Q
. In particular, if
A
is an alphabet, then
(2^{A^{*}},\cup)
is a rating algebra. In practice, these are the only
infinite
rating algebras that we shall consider.

We often apply a “downset operator” to subsets of our rating algebras
R
. That is, for every
S\subseteq R
, we write
\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}S
for the set
\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}S=\bigl\{r\mid\exists s\in S\text{ such that }r\leq s\bigr\}
. We also consider Cartesian products
X\times R
of an arbitrary set
X
with a rating algebra
R
. Given
S\subseteq X\times R
, we write
\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}S=\{(x,r)\in X\times R\mid\text{$\exists r^{\prime}\in R$ such that $r\leq r^{\prime}$ and $(x,r^{\prime})\in S$}\}
.

Definition of a rating map.
As seen above,
(2^{A^{*}},\cup)
is a rating algebra. A
rating map
(over
A
) is a monoid morphism
\rho:(2^{A^{*}},\cup)\to(R,+)
where
(R,+)
is an arbitrary
finite
rating algebra. In other words, we have
\rho(\emptyset)=0_{R}
and
\rho(K_{1}\cup K_{2})=\rho(K_{1})+\rho(K_{2})
for all
K_{1},K_{2}\subseteq A^{*}
. Note that since rating maps are morphisms of rating algebras, they are necessarily increasing: if
K_{1}\subseteq K_{2}
, then
\rho(K_{1})\leq\rho(K_{2})
. For the sake of improved readability, when applying a rating map
\rho
to a singleton language
K=\{w\}
(
i.e.
,
w\in A^{*}
is a word), we write
\rho(w)
for
\rho(\{w\})
. We often consider rating maps satisfying additional properties.

Nice rating maps.
We say that a rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
is
nice
to indicate that for every language
K\subseteq A^{*}
, there exists a
finite
set
F\subseteq K
such that
\rho(K)=\rho(F)
.

Remark 8.2
.

Not all rating maps are nice. Consider the rating algebra
R=\{0,1\}
whose addition is defined by
i+j=\mathord{\text{max}}(i,j)
for
i,j\in R
. We define
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
by
\rho(K)=0
if
K\subseteq A^{*}
is
finite
and
\rho(K)=1
if
K\subseteq A^{*}
is
infinite
. One may verify that
\rho
is
not
nice: if
K
is infinite, then
\rho(K)=1
while
\rho(F)=0
for every finite subset
F\subseteq K
.

The definition of nice rating maps motivates the following object. For every rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
(nice or not), we associate a map
\rho_{*}:A^{*}\to R
defined as the restriction of
\rho
to
A^{*}
: for every
w\in A^{*}
,
\rho_{*}(w)=\rho(w)
. One may verify that when
\rho
is nice, it is characterized by
\rho_{*}
. More precisely, for every
K\subseteq A^{*}
, we have
\rho(K)=\sum_{w\in K}\rho_{*}(w)
(the sum is well-defined as it boils down to a finite one since
\rho
is nice and
R
is idempotent and commutative).

Multiplicative rating maps.
The rating algebras of multiplicative rating maps have more structure: they are
idempotent semirings
. A
semiring
is a tuple
(R,+,\cdot)
where
R
is a set and “
+
” and “
\cdot
” are two binary operations, such that the following axioms are satisfied:

•

(R,+)
is a commutative monoid (its neutral element is denoted by
0_{R}
).

•

(R,\cdot)
is a monoid (its neutral element is denoted by
1_{R}
).

•

The neutral element of
(R,+)
is a zero for multiplication:
0_{R}r=r0_{R}=0_{R}
for all
r\in R
.

•

The multiplication distributes over addition:
r(s+t)=rs+rt
and
(r+s)t=rt+st
for every
r,s,t\in R
.

Finally, a semiring
(R,+,\cdot)
is
idempotent
when
r+r=r
for every
r\in R
(on the other hand, there is no additional constraint on the multiplication). By definition, it follows that in this case, the additive monoid
(R,+)
is a rating algebra.

Example 8.3
.

For every alphabet
A
, the triple
(2^{A^{*}},\cup,\cdot)
is an idempotent semiring (here, we use language concatenation “
\cdot
” as the multiplication; its neutral element is the singleton
\{\varepsilon\}
).

A rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
is
multiplicative
when the rating algebra
(R,+)
is equipped with a second binary operation “
\cdot
” such that
(R,+,\cdot)
is an
idempotent semiring
and
\rho
is also a monoid morphism from
(2^{A^{*}},\cdot)
to
(R,\cdot)
. Thus, the axioms are as follows:

(1)

\rho(\emptyset)=0_{R}
and for all
K_{1},K_{2}\subseteq A^{*}
, we have
\rho(K_{1}\cup K_{2})=\rho(K_{1})+\rho(K_{2})
.

(2)

\rho(\varepsilon)=1_{R}
and for all
K_{1},K_{2}\subseteq A^{*}
, we have
\rho(K_{1}K_{2})=\rho(K_{1})\cdot\rho(K_{2})
.

Altogether, this exactly says that
\rho
is a semiring morphism from
(2^{A^{*}},\cup,\cdot)
to
(R,+,\cdot)
.

A key point is that a rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to(R,+,\cdot)
that is both
nice
and
multiplicative
can be finitely represented. Indeed, since
\rho
is nice, it is characterized by the map
\rho_{*}:A^{*}\to R
. Moreover, since
\rho
is multiplicative,
\rho_{*}
is a monoid morphism from
A^{*}
to
(R,\cdot)
. Altogether, it follows that
\rho
is finitely representable: it suffices to store the image
\rho(a)\in R
of each letter
a\in A
as well as the addition and multiplication tables of
(R,+,\cdot)
. This means that we can speak of algorithms
taking a nice multiplicative rating map as input
.

Canonical nice multiplicative rating map associated to a morphism.
We complete the presentation with a simple construction. It associates a canonical nice multiplicative rating map to a morphism into a finite monoid. We shall use it to make the link with covering. Consider a morphism
\alpha:A^{*}\to M
into a finite monoid. We associate a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho_{\alpha}:2^{A^{*}}\to 2^{M}
to
\alpha
. Consider the triple
(2^{M},\cup,\cdot)
whose multiplication is defined as follows for all
T,T^{\prime}\in 2^{M}
:

TT^{\prime}=\{tt^{\prime}\in M\mid\text{$t\in T$ and $t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}$}\}.

One may verify that
(2^{M},\cup,\cdot)
is an idempotent semiring. For every
K\subseteq A^{*}
, we define,

\rho_{\alpha}(K)=\alpha(K)=\{t\in M\mid\alpha^{-1}(t)\cap K\neq\emptyset\}.

One may verify that
\rho_{\alpha}:2^{A^{*}}\to 2^{M}
is a
nice multiplicative rating map
.

8.2.
Optimal covers and optimal imprints.

Now that we have defined what rating maps are, we turn to imprints. Consider a rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
. Given any finite set of languages
\mathbf{K}
, we define the
\rho
-imprint of
\mathbf{K}
. Roughly speaking, when
\mathbf{K}
is a cover of some language
L
, this object measures the “quality” of
\mathbf{K}
, which is a subset of
R
. Intuitively, the smaller the imprint, the better the cover. The
\rho
-imprint of
\mathbf{K}

is the subset of
R
defined by:

\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})=\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\big\{\rho(K)\mid K\in\mathbf{K}\big\}.

We now define optimality. Consider an arbitrary rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
and a lattice
\mathscr{D}
. Given a language
L
, an
optimal

\mathscr{D}
-cover of
L
for
\rho
is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover
\mathbf{K}
of
L
having the least possible imprint among all
\mathscr{D}
-covers,
i.e.
, which satisfies the following property:

\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K}^{\prime})\quad\text{for every $\mathscr{D}$-cover $\mathbf{K}^{\prime}$ of $L$}.

In general, there can be infinitely many optimal
\mathscr{D}
-covers for a given rating map
\rho
. The key point is that there always exists at least one, provided that
\mathscr{D}
is a lattice. We state this simple property in the following lemma (proved in
Place & Zeitoun 2018
, Lemma 4.15
).

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a lattice. For every language
L
and every rating map
\rho
, there exists an optimal
\mathscr{D}
-cover of
L
for
\rho
.

Clearly, given a lattice
\mathscr{D}
, a language
L
and a rating map
\rho
, all optimal
\mathscr{D}
-covers of
L
for
\rho
have the same
\rho
-imprint. Hence, this unique
\rho
-imprint is a
canonical
object for
\mathscr{D}
,
L
and
\rho
. We call it the
optimal
\rho
-imprint on
L
for
\mathscr{D}

and we denote it by
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[L,\rho]
:

\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[L,\rho]=\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})\quad\text{for any optimal $\mathscr{D}$-cover $\mathbf{K}$ of $L$ for $\rho$}.

An important special case is when
L=A^{*}
. In this case, we write
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
for
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[A^{*},\rho]
. Let us present a few properties of optimal imprints. First, we have the following useful fact (proved in
Place & Zeitoun 2018
, Facts 4.16 and 4.17
).

Fact 8.5
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be lattices such that
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
,
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a rating map and
H,L\subseteq A^{*}
be two languages such that
H\subseteq L
. Then,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[H,\rho]\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{C}}[L,\rho]
.

Additionally, we have the following lemma (proved in
Place & Zeitoun 2021
, Fact 4.7
).

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a lattice and let
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a rating map. Then,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\emptyset,\rho]=\emptyset
. Moreover, given two languages
H,L
, we have
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[H\cup L,\rho]=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[H,\rho]\cup\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[L,\rho]

We complete Lemma
8.6
with a similar statement for language concatenation instead of union (proved in
Place & Zeitoun 2018
, Lemma 5.8
). Note that it requires more hypotheses:
\mathscr{D}
must be a prevariety and the rating map
\rho
must be multiplicative.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a prevariety and let
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a multiplicative rating map. Given two languages
H,L\subseteq A^{*}
,
we have
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[H,\rho]\cdot\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[L,\rho]\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[HL,\rho]
.

8.3.
Connection with covering.

We now connect these definitions to the covering problem. The key idea is that solving
\mathscr{D}
-covering for a class
\mathscr{D}
boils down to finding an algorithm that computes the optimal imprint for
\mathscr{D}
from a nice multiplicative rating map given as input. In
Place & Zeitoun 2018
, two statements are presented. The first is simpler but it only applies Boolean algebras, while the second, more involved, applies to all lattices. Since all classes investigated in the paper are Boolean algebras, we only present the first one.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a Boolean algebra. There exists an effective reduction from
\mathscr{D}
-covering to the following problem:

Input:

A nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
and
F\subseteq R
.

Question:

Is it true that
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]\cap F=\emptyset
?

Proof sketch.

We briefly describe the reduction (we refer the reader to
Place & Zeitoun 2018
for details). Consider an input pair
(L_{0},\{L_{1},\dots,L_{n}\})
for
\mathscr{D}
-covering. Since the languages
L_{i}
are regular, for every
i\leq n
, one may compute a morphism
\alpha_{i}:A^{*}\to M_{i}
into a finite monoid recognizing
L_{i}
together with the set
F_{i}\subseteq M_{i}
such that
L_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{-1}(F_{i})
. Consider the associated nice multiplicative rating maps
\rho_{\alpha_{i}}:2^{A^{*}}\to 2^{M_{i}}
. Moreover, let
R
be the idempotent semiring
2^{M_{0}}\times\cdots\times 2^{M_{n}}
equipped with the componentwise addition and multiplication. We define a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
by letting
\rho(K)=(\rho_{\alpha_{0}}(K),\dots,\rho_{\alpha_{n}}(K))
for every
K\subseteq A^{*}
. Finally, let
F\subseteq R
be the set of all tuples
(X_{0},\dots,X_{n})\in R
such that
X_{i}\cap F_{i}\neq\emptyset
for every
i\leq n
. One may now verify that
(L_{0},\{L_{1},\dots,L_{n}\})
is
\mathscr{D}
-coverable if and only if
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]\cap F=\emptyset
. Let us point out that this equivalence is only true when
\mathscr{D}
is a Boolean algebra. When
\mathscr{D}
is only a lattice, one has to handle the language
L_{0}
separately.
∎

In view of Proposition
8.8
, for a Boolean algebra
\mathscr{D}
, getting a
\mathscr{D}
-covering algorithm boils down to finding a procedure computing the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]\subseteq R
from a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
. In practice, these procedures are often presented as elegant
characterization theorems
. The key idea is that such a theorem should provide a description of the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]\subseteq R
, which yields an algorithm for computing it as an immediate corollary. Typically, such a result is called a “characterization of optimal imprints for
\mathscr{D}
”. For example a characterization of optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}
is presented in
Place & Zeitoun 2018
(recall that
\mathit{SF}
is the class of star-free languages). This procedure reformulates a result of
Place & Zeitoun 2016
in the framework of rating maps.

Example 8.9
.

It is shown in
Place & Zeitoun 2016
;
Place & Zeitoun 2018
that for every nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
, the optimal
\rho
-imprint for
\mathit{SF}
,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}}[\rho]\subseteq R
, is the least subset
S\subseteq R
which satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

Trivial elements:
For every
w\in A^{*}
, we have
\rho(w)\in S
.

(2)

Closure under downset.

\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}S=S
.

(3)

Closure under multiplication.
For every
q,r\in S
, we have
qr\in S
.

(4)

\mathit{SF}
-closure.
For every
r\in S
, we have
r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1}\in S
.

This characterization yields a
least fixpoint procedure
that computes
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}}[\rho]
from
\rho
: it starts from the set of all trivial elements and saturates it with the operations given by conditions (
2
)–(
4
) above. Together with Proposition
8.8
, this yields the decidability of
\mathit{SF}
-covering.

In the paper, our aim is to generalize the characterization presented in Example
8.9
to arbitrary classes of the form
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
where
\mathscr{C}
is a prevariety. We are able to handle two cases: the case when
\mathscr{C}
is a
finite prevariety
and the case when
\mathscr{C}
is a
group prevariety
. We present two characterizations of optimal imprints, one for each case. They are generic in the sense that each of them applies to families of classes rather than to a single class. This raises a question. Intuitively, a generic characterization of optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
should be parametrized by the class
\mathscr{C}
. How is this achieved? It turns out that this requires to work with
more general objects
capturing
additional information
. Roughly speaking, given a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
, our characterizations describe a single object that captures both
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]\subseteq R
and
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{C}}[\rho]\subseteq R
, as well as extra information, which connects them. The key idea is that while we are only interested in the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]
, this more general object is required to formulate a characterization. This is not surprising since characterizations of optimal imprints are often
fixpoint descriptions
, as seen in Example
8.9
. An important point is that these more general objects are also optimal imprints. However, they involve auxiliary rating maps built from
\mathscr{C}
and
\rho
. The last part of the section is devoted to defining these objects.

8.4.
Nested optimal imprints

We introduce a construction from
Place 2018
;
Place & Zeitoun 2021
, which takes as input a lattice
\mathscr{D}
and a rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
and builds a new rating map
\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
whose rating algebra is
(2^{R},\cup)
. We let,

\begin{array}[]{llll}\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]:&2^{A^{*}}&\to&(2^{R},\cup)\\
&K&\mapsto&\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[K,\rho].\end{array}

Let us reformulate Lemma
8.6
, which exactly states that
\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]:2^{A^{*}}\to(2^{R},\cup)
is itself a
rating map
.

Corollary 8.10
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a lattice and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
a rating map., Then,
\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]:2^{A^{*}}\to 2^{R}
is a rating map as well.

Let us point out that
\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
is neither nice nor multiplicative in general, even if this is the case for the original rating map
\rho
. In practice, this is not an issue for the “multiplicative” property. Actually,
\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
is multiplicative when
\rho
is multiplicative and the lattice
\mathscr{D}
is a prevariety
closed under concatenation
(such as when
\mathscr{D}=\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
). On the other hand,
\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
is rarely nice (see
Place & Zeitoun 2021
, Example 6.3
for a counterexample). This is why it is important that most results of the framework hold for
arbitrary rating maps
.

Let us now provide some high-level intuition on why this construction is important when dealing with star-free closure. Consider some input prevariety
\mathscr{C}
. Since
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
is a Boolean algebra, we know by Proposition
8.8
that in order to solve
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-covering, it suffices to characterize the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]\subseteq R
for every nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
. Roughly speaking, the two characterizations of optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
that we present (for finite prevarieties
\mathscr{C}
in Section
9
and for prevarieties of group languages in Section
10
) consider the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{C}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]]\subseteq 2^{R}
, which is more general. Indeed, one may verify that
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]
is the union of all sets in
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{C}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]]
. The point is that the extra information contained in this set is required in order to formulate these characterizations. Let us point out that this discussion is only meant to provide a sketchy general intuition. In practice, we shall refine these ideas by adopting an approach tailored to the two particular kinds of input prevarieties that we shall consider.

9.
Covering for finite input classes

In this section, we prove that separation and covering are both decidable for the class
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
when
\mathscr{C}
is a finite prevariety. The algorithm is based on the framework introduced in Section
8
: we present a generic effective characterization of optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. Given as input an arbitrary
finite
prevariety
\mathscr{C}
and a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
, it provides an effective description of the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]\subseteq R
. By Proposition
8.8
, having this set in hand suffices to decide
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-covering. As announced at the end of Section
8
, the characterization actually describes more information than just the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]\subseteq R
. The first part of the section is devoted to defining the full object that we characterize. In the second part, we present the characterization itself and its proof.

9.1.
Pointed optimal imprints

Consider an arbitrary finite prevariety
\mathscr{C}
. Recall that since
\mathscr{C}
is
finite
, Lemma
2.5
implies that there exists a
unique
(up to renaming)
\mathscr{C}
-morphism recognizing exactly all languages in
\mathscr{C}
. We denote it by
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}:A^{*}\to N_{\mathscr{C}}
and call it the
canonical
\mathscr{C}
-morphism
. The set
\mathbf{K}=\{\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(t)\mid t\in N_{\mathscr{C}}\}
is the
finest
partition of
A^{*}
into languages of
\mathscr{C}
. Consequently,
\mathbf{K}
is an optimal
\mathscr{C}
-cover of
A^{*}

for all rating maps
. In particular, if
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
is a nice multiplicative rating map, then
\mathbf{K}
is an optimal
\mathscr{C}
-cover of
A^{*}
for the auxiliary rating map
\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]:2^{A^{*}}\to 2^{R}
. By definition, it follows that,

\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{C}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]]=\mathscr{I}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]](\mathbf{K})=\mathord{\downarrow_{2^{R}}}\{\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho](K)\mid K\in\mathbf{K}\}=\mathord{\downarrow_{2^{R}}}\{\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(t),\rho]\mid t\in N_{\mathscr{C}}\}.

As explained in Section
8
, the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{C}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]]
is exactly the information that our characterization of optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
will describe. More precisely, we characterize the family of sets
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(t),\rho]\subseteq R
for
t\in N_{\mathscr{C}}
. According to Lemma
8.6
, the union of these sets is
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[A^{*},\rho]=\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]
, whose knowledge is enough to decide
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-covering. Let us point out that this is also how the characterization depends on the finite prevariety
\mathscr{C}
: it is parametrized by the canonical
\mathscr{C}
-morphism
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}:A^{*}\to N_{\mathscr{C}}
. We now introduce additional notations that will be convenient in order to manipulate the family of sets
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(t),\rho]
in our statements and our proofs.

Definition.
Let
\mathscr{D}
be a lattice,
\eta:A^{*}\to N
be a morphism into a finite monoid and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a rating map. The
\eta
-pointed optimal
\rho
-imprint for
\mathscr{D}

is defined as the following set
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]\subseteq N\times R
:

\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]=\bigl\{(t,r)\in N\times R\mid r\in\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta^{-1}(t),\rho]\bigl\}.

Clearly,
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]
encodes all sets
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta^{-1}(t),\rho]
for
t\in N
. We shall use the notation in the case when
\mathscr{D}=\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
for some prevariety
\mathscr{C}
and
\eta
is the canonical
\mathscr{C}
-morphism
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}
.

We complete the definition with a simple result which implies that
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]\subseteq N\times R
is more general than the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]\subseteq R
. Indeed, since
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[A^{*},\rho]
, we have the following immediate corollary of Lemma
8.6
.

Corollary 9.1
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a lattice,
\eta:A^{*}\to N
be a morphism into a finite monoid and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a multiplicative rating map. Then,

\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]=\bigcup_{t\in N}\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta^{-1}(t),\rho]=\{r\in R\mid\text{there exists $t\in N$ such that $(t,r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]$}\}.

Pointed covers.
Pointed optimal imprints are closer to being a notation rather than a new notion. Yet, it is possible to define them
directly
in terms of “covers”. This will be convenient for manipulating them. However, we have to slightly generalize the notion of cover in order to do so.

Consider a morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to N
into a finite monoid and a language
L\subseteq A^{*}
. An
\eta
-pointed cover
of
L
is a
finite
set
\mathbf{K}
of pairs
(s,K)\in N\times 2^{A^{*}}
such that for every
w\in L
, there exists
(s,K)\in\mathbf{K}
such that
\eta(w)=s
and
w\in K
. In other words, the set
\{K\mid(s,K)\in\mathbf{K}\}
must be a cover of
L\cap\eta^{-1}(s)
for every
s\in N
. Additionally, given some class
\mathscr{D}
, we say that
\mathbf{K}
is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathscr{D}
-cover when it also satisfies
K\in\mathscr{D}
for each pair
(s,K)\in\mathbf{K}
.

We generalize imprints to pointed covers. Let
\eta:A^{*}\to N
be a morphism into a finite monoid and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
a be rating map. If
\mathbf{K}
is an
\eta
-pointed cover of some language
L\subseteq A^{*}
, we write,

\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})=\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\{(s,\rho(K))\mid(s,K)\in\mathbf{K}\}\subseteq N\times R.

Here, we use the extended definition of the downset operation (see the definition page
8.1
). The following lemma provides an alternate definition of pointed optimal imprints. Roughly, it implies that when
\mathscr{D}
is a lattice, there always exists an “optimal”
\eta
-pointed
\mathscr{D}
-cover
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
(
i.e.
, such that
\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})
is minimal for inclusion) and that it satisfies
\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})=\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]
.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a lattice,
\eta:A^{*}\to N
be a morphism into a finite monoid and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a rating map. The two following properties hold:

•

For every
\eta
-pointed
\mathscr{D}
-cover
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
, we have
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]\subseteq\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})
.

•

There exists an
\eta
-pointed
\mathscr{D}
-cover
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
such that
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]=\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})
.

Proof.

For the first assertion, let
\mathbf{K}
be an
\eta
-pointed
\mathscr{D}
-cover
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
and let
(s,r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]
. Let
\mathbf{K}_{s}=\{K\mid(s,K)\in\mathbf{K}\}
, which is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover of
\eta^{-1}(s)
by definition of pointed covers. Since
(s,r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]
, we have
r\in\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta^{-1}(s),\rho]
, which yields
K\in\mathbf{K}_{s}
such that
r\leq\rho(K)
. By definition of
\mathbf{K}_{s}
, we have
(s,K)\in\mathbf{K}
. Hence, we get
(s,r)\in\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})
, as desired.

We turn to the second assertion. For every
s\in N
, let
\mathbf{K}_{s}
be an optimal
\mathscr{D}
-cover of
\eta^{-1}(s)
for
\rho
:
\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K}_{s})=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta^{-1}(s),\rho]
. We let
\mathbf{K}=\{(s,K)\mid s\in N\text{ and }K\in\mathbf{K}_{s}\}
. By definition,
\mathbf{K}
is an
\eta
-pointed cover of
L
. Let us prove that
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta,\rho]=\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})
. The left to right inclusion is immediate from the first assertion. For the converse one, let
(s,r)\in\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})
. By definition of
\mathbf{K}
, we get
K\in\mathbf{K}_{s}
such that
r\leq\rho(K)
,
i.e.
,
r\in\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K}_{s})=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\eta^{-1}(s),\rho]
. Hence,
(s,r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{C}}[\eta,\rho]
by definition.
∎

9.2.
Characterization

Let us first present the characterization. Given an arbitrary morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to N
into a finite monoid and a multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
, we define the
\mathit{SF}
-saturated subsets of
N\times R
for
\eta
and
\rho
(this notion makes sense for arbitrary multiplicative rating maps, but it is only useful for those that are
nice
). Let
S\subseteq N\times R
. We say that
S
is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated for
\eta
and
\rho
when it satisfies the following properties:

(1)

Trivial elements.
For every
w\in A^{*}
, we have
(\eta(w),\rho(w))\in S
.

(2)

Closure under downset.

\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}S=S
.

(3)

Closure under multiplication.
For every
(s,q),(t,r)\in S
, we have
(st,qr)\in S
.

(4)

\mathit{SF}
-closure.
For every
(e,r)\in S
, if
e\in N
is an idempotent, then
(e,r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1})\in S
.

We are ready to present the characterization. Given a finite prevariety
\mathscr{C}
, if
\rho
is nice, we show that the least
\mathit{SF}
-saturated subset of
N_{\mathscr{C}}\times R
for
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\rho
is exactly
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a finite prevariety and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a nice multiplicative rating map. Then,
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
is the least
\mathit{SF}
-saturated subset of
N_{\mathscr{C}}\times R
for
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\rho
.

Given a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
as input, it is clear the one may compute the least
\mathit{SF}
-saturated subset of
N_{\mathscr{C}}\times R
for
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\rho
. This is achieved with a least fixpoint procedure. Hence, Theorem
9.3
provides an algorithm for computing
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
. It then follows from Corollary
9.1
that one may compute
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]
from
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
:

\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\rho]=\{r\in R\mid\text{there exists $t\in N$ such that $(t,r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]$}\}.

Together with Proposition
8.8
, we obtain that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-covering is decidable. Naturally, this result extends to separation by Lemma
2.2
. Altogether, we get the following corollary.

Corollary 9.4
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a finite prevariety. Then,
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-covering and
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-separation are decidable.

In practice, there are not many interesting applications of Corollary
9.4
. Indeed, the only important class that is the star-free closure of a finite prevariety is the original class of star-free languages. Indeed, we have
\mathit{SF}=\mathit{SF}(\textup{ST})
where
\textup{ST}=\{\emptyset,A^{*}\}
. It was already know that
\mathit{SF}
has decidable covering: we presented an effective characterization of optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}
taken from
Place & Zeitoun 2018
in Example
8.9
. This specialized characterization is actually an immediate corollary of Theorem
9.3
since the canonical ST-morphism is the unique one
\eta_{\textup{ST}}:A^{*}\to\{1\}
into a trivial monoid
\{1\}
.

Nonetheless, Theorem
9.3
is an important result. Indeed, we shall use it as a subresult in the proof of our second characterization, which describes optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
when
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety (more precisely, we shall apply Propositions
9.5
and
9.6
below).

We turn to the proof of Theorem
9.3
. It involves two independent statements, which correspond respectively to soundness and completeness of the least fixpoint procedure computing
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
from a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho
. Let us start with soundness, which is simpler to establish and does not require the hypothesis that
\rho
is nice.

Proposition 9.5

(Soundness)
.

Consider a finite prevariety
\mathscr{C}
and a multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
. Then, the set
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]\subseteq N_{\mathscr{C}}\times R
is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated for
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\rho
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
is a prevariety by Proposition
3.2
. There are four properties to verify. We start with the first three, which are standard. For the trivial elements, consider
w\in A^{*}
and let
\mathbf{K}
be an optimal
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-cover of
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(\eta_{\mathscr{C}}(w))
. Since
w\in\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(\eta_{\mathscr{C}}(w))
, there exists
K\in\mathbf{K}
such that
w\in K
. Therefore,
\rho(w)\leq\rho(K)
, which yields
\rho(w)\in\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})=\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(\eta_{\mathscr{C}}(w)),\rho]
. By definition, this implies that
(\eta_{\mathscr{C}}(w),\rho(w))\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
. Closure under downset is immediate by definition of imprints. Finally, for closure under multiplication, consider
(s_{1},r_{1}),(s_{2},r_{2})\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
. We have
r_{i}\in\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(s_{i}),\rho]
for
i=1,2
. Since
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
is a prevariety, Lemma
8.7
yields
r_{1}r_{2}\in\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(s_{1})\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(s_{2}),\rho]
. Clearly,
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(s_{1})\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(s_{2})\subseteq\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(s_{1}s_{2})
. Thus, Fact
8.5
yields
r_{1}r_{2}\in\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(s_{1}s_{2}),\rho]
. By definition, this exactly says that
(s_{1}s_{2},r_{1}r_{2})\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
.

It remains to handle
\mathit{SF}
-closure. Let
(e,r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
be such that
e\in N_{\mathscr{C}}
is idempotent. We show that
(e,r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1})\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
. Let
\mathbf{K}
be an optimal
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-cover of
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(e)
. By definition, it now suffices to prove that
r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1}\in\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})
. Since
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
is a prevariety, Proposition
2.4
yields an
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-morphism
\alpha:A^{*}\to M
recognizing every
K\in\mathbf{K}
. Let
\mathbf{H}
be the set of all languages
\alpha^{-1}(x)
for
x\in M
such that
\alpha^{-1}(x)\cap\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(e)\neq\emptyset
. By definition,
\mathbf{H}
is an
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-cover of
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(e)
. Hence, since
(e,r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
, we have
r\in\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{H})
which yields
H\in\mathbf{H}
such that
r\leq\rho(H)
. By definition of
\mathbf{H}
, we get
x\in M
such that
H=\alpha^{-1}(x)
and some word
u\in H\cap\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(e)
. Let
p=\omega(M)\times\omega(R)
and
L=\alpha^{-1}(x^{p})
. We claim that:

(13)

H^{p}\cup H^{p+1}\subseteq L.

Let us first explain why this implies that
r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1}\in\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})
. Since
u^{p}\in\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(e)
(recall that
e\in N_{\mathscr{C}}
is idempotent) and
\mathbf{K}
is a cover of
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}^{-1}(e)
, we get
K\in\mathbf{K}
such that
u^{p}\in K
. Moreover, since
u^{p}\in L=\alpha^{-1}(x^{p})
and
K
is recognized by
\alpha
(by definition), it follows that
L\subseteq K
. Hence, since
r\leq\rho(H)
and
p=\omega(M)\times\omega(R)
, it follows from (
13
) that
r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1}\leq\rho(K)
which yields
r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1}\in\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})
, as desired.

It remains to prove (
13
). Let
w\in H^{p}\cup H^{p+1}
. We need to prove that
\alpha(w)=x^{p}
. This is immediate when
w\in H^{p}
since
H=\alpha^{-1}(x)
. Assume now that
w\in H^{p+1}
. In that case, we have
\alpha(w)=x^{p+1}
. Hence, it suffices to prove that
x^{p+1}=x^{p}
. By hypothesis
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}(u)=e
is an idempotent of
N_{\mathscr{C}}
. Since
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}
is the canonical
\mathscr{C}
-morphism, it follows from Lemma
2.5
that the image of
u
under
any

\mathscr{C}
-morphism is an idempotent. Hence, since
\alpha
is an
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-morphism, Proposition
3.3
implies that
(\alpha(u))^{\omega+1}=(\alpha(u))^{\omega}
. Since
x=\alpha(u)
and
p=\omega(M)\times\omega(R)
, it follows that
x^{p+1}=x^{p}
, as desired.
∎

We now turn to completeness. As usual, this is the most difficult part of the proof. Note that again, we shall rely on Theorem
4.10
: we build our languages in
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
using the operations available in the definition of
\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
.

Proposition 9.6

(Completeness)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a prevariety,
\eta:A^{*}\to N
be a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism,
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a
nice
multiplicative rating map and
S\subseteq N\times R
be an
\mathit{SF}
-saturated set for
\eta
and
\rho
. There exists an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-cover
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
such that
\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq S
.

Proof.

We build the
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-cover
\mathbf{K}
using induction. Let us start with some terminology. A first point is that we build particular
\eta
-pointed covers. Let
P\subseteq A^{*}
be an arbitrary language. An
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
of
P
is a
finite
set
\mathbf{H}\subseteq N\times 2^{A^{*}}
such that for every
(t,H)\in\mathbf{H}
, we have
H\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
and for every
t\in N
, the set
\{H\mid(t,H)\in\mathbf{H}\}
is a
partition
of
P\cap\eta^{-1}(t)
(this implies that
H\subseteq\eta^{-1}(t)
for every
(t,H)\in\mathbf{H}
). Note that in particular,
\mathbf{H}
is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-cover of
P
.

If
\mathbf{H}
is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition, an
\mathbf{H}
-product
is a pair
(t_{1}\cdots t_{n},H_{1}\cdots H_{n})
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
where
(t_{i},H_{i})\in\mathbf{H}
for every
i\leq n
. In particular, the pair
(1_{N},\{\varepsilon\})
is an
\mathbf{H}
-product: this is the case
n=0
in the definition. When
n\geq 1
, we speak of
strict
\mathbf{H}
-products
. Finally, an
\mathbf{H}
-union
(resp. strict
\mathbf{H}
-union
) is a pair
(t,G_{1}\cup\cdots\cup G_{m})
for some
t\in N
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(t,G_{j})
is an
\mathbf{H}
-product (resp. strict
\mathbf{H}
-product) for every
j\leq m
. Note that
(t,\emptyset)
is a strict
\mathbf{H}
-union for every
t\in\mathbb{N}
: this corresponds to the case
m=0
in the definition.

Additionally, we write
Q=N\times R
. Observe that
Q
is a monoid for the componentwise multiplication. Moreover,
S\subseteq Q
by definition. Finally, for every
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
\mathbf{H}
, we associate two subsets of
Q
. The definitions are as follows:

•

We let
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}\subseteq Q
be the set of all elements
(t,\rho(H))\in Q
where
(t,H)
is a
strict

\mathbf{H}
-union.

•

We let
Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\subseteq Q
be the set of all elements
(t,\rho(H))\in Q
where
(t,H)
is an
\mathbf{H}
-union.

Clearly,
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}\subseteq Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
. Moreover, we have the following simple fact which we shall use implicitly throughout the proof.

Fact 9.7
.

Let
P\subseteq A^{*}
and
\mathbf{H}
be an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
P
. For all
(t_{1},q_{1}),(t_{2},q_{2})\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
, we have
(t_{1}t_{2},q_{1}q_{2})\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
.

Proof.

By definition, there are two
\mathbf{H}
-unions
(t_{1},H_{1}),(t_{2},H_{2})\in\mathbf{H}
such that
q_{i}=\rho(H_{i})
for
i=1,2
. One may verify from the definition that
(t_{1}t_{2},H_{1}H_{2})
remains an
\mathbf{H}
-union since language distributes over union. Since
q_{1}q_{2}=\rho(H_{1}H_{2})
, it follows that
(t_{1}t_{2},q_{1}q_{2})\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
.
∎

We are ready to prove Proposition
9.6
. It is based on the following statement.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
P\subseteq A^{+}
be a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay and
\mathbf{H}
be an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
P
such that
(t,\rho(H))\in S
for every
(t,H)\in\mathbf{H}
. Then, for every
(s,r)\in S
, there exists an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
such that,

(14)

for every
(t,K)\in\mathbf{K}
, we have
(t,\rho(K))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
and
(st,r\rho(K))\in S
.

Before we prove Lemma
9.8
, let us first complete the main argument. We have to build an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-cover
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
such that
\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq S
. Observe that
A\subseteq A^{+}
is a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay. Moreover,
\mathbf{H}=\{(\eta(a),\{a\})\mid a\in A\}
is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
A
and we have
(\eta(a),\rho(a))\in S
for every
a\in A
since
S
is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated (these are trivial elements). Finally,
(1_{N},1_{R})\in S
(again, this is a trivial element). Therefore, we may apply Lemma
9.8
in the case when
P=A
and
(s,r)=(1_{N},1_{R})\in S
. This yields an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
satisfying (
14
). In particular,
(t,\rho(K))\in S
for every
(t,K)\in\mathbf{K}
. Therefore, since
S
is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated, closure under downset yields
\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq S
, which completes the proof.

We now prove Lemma
9.8
. Let
P\subseteq A^{+}
be a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay and
\mathbf{H}
be an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
P
such that
(t,\rho(H))\in S
for all
(t,H)\in\mathbf{H}
. Finally, let
(s,r)\in S
. We need to build an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
satisfying (
14
). We proceed by induction on the three following parameters, listed by order of importance:

(1)

The size of the set
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}\subseteq Q
,

(2)

The size of
\mathbf{H}
,

(3)

The size of the set
(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\subseteq Q
.

We distinguish two main cases depending on the following property. We say that
(s,r)
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable
when the following holds,

(15)

\text{for every $(t,H)\in\mathbf{H}$,}\quad(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}=(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H)).

We first consider the case when
(s,r)
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable. This is the base case: we construct
\mathbf{K}
directly. Then, we handle the case when
(s,r)
is not
\mathbf{H}
-stable using induction on our three parameters.

Base case:
(s,r)
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable.
In this case, we let
\mathbf{K}=\{(t,P^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t))\mid t\in N\}
. Let us first verify that this is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
. It is immediate that
\{P^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)\}
is a partition of
P^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)
for every
t\in N
. Moreover, we have
P^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
for every
t\in N
. Indeed, we have
P\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
: it is the disjoint union of all languages involved in the
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{H}
of
P
. Since
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})=\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
by Theorem
4.10
, we obtain
P^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
since
P
is a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay and
\eta^{-1}(t)\in\mathscr{C}
(recall that
\eta
is a
\mathscr{C}
-morphism by hypothesis). It remains to prove that
\mathbf{K}
satisfies (
14
): for every
(t,K)\in\mathbf{K}
, we show that
(t,\rho(K))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
and
(st,r\rho(K))\in S
. We start with the former property (this is where we use the hypothesis that
\rho
is nice).

Fact 9.9
.

For every
(t,K)\in\mathbf{K}
, we have
(t,\rho(K))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
.

Proof.

By definition of
\mathbf{K}
, we have
K=P^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)
. Since
\mathbf{H}
is an
\eta
-pointed partition of
P
, one may verify that
P^{*}\cap\eta^{-1}(t)
is the (infinite) union of
all

\mathbf{H}
-products
(t^{\prime},H)
such that
t^{\prime}=t
. Since
\rho
is nice, it follows that there exists
finitely many

\mathbf{H}
-products
(t,H_{1}),\dots,(t,H_{\ell})
such that
\rho(K)=\rho(H_{1})+\cdots+\rho(H_{\ell})=\rho(H_{1}\cup\cdots\cup H_{\ell})
. Clearly,
(t,H_{1}\cup\cdots\cup H_{\ell})
is an
\mathbf{H}
-union and it follows that
(t,\rho(K))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
, as desired.
∎

It remains to show that
(st,r\rho(K))\in S
for every
K\in\mathbf{K}
. We prove that for every
(t,q)\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
, we have
(st,rq)\in S
. In view of Fact
9.9
, this yields the desired result. First, we use the hypothesis that
(s,r)
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable to prove the following fact.

Fact 9.10
.

Let
(e,H)
be an
\mathbf{H}
-product such that
(e,\rho(H))\in Q
is a pair of idempotents. For every
(t,q)\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
, we have
(ste,rq\rho(H))=(st,rq)
.

Proof.

We first use the hypothesis that
(s,r)
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable to prove the following preliminary result which holds regardless of whether
(e,\rho(H))
is a pair of idempotents or not:

(16)

\text{there exists $(x,y)\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}$ such that $(sxe,ry\rho(H))=(st,rq)$}.

Since
(e,H)
is an
\mathbf{H}
-product, we can find elements
(t^{\prime}_{1},H_{1}),\dots,(t^{\prime}_{n},H_{n})
of
\mathbf{H}
such that
(e,H)=(t^{\prime}_{1}\cdots t^{\prime}_{n},H_{1}\cdots H_{n})
. We proceed by induction on
n
. If
n=0
, then
e=1_{N}
and
H=\{\varepsilon\}
. It suffices to choose
(x,y)=(t,q)\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
. We now assume that
n\geq 1
. By induction, we get
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
such that
(sx^{\prime}t^{\prime}_{2}\cdots t^{\prime}_{n},ry^{\prime}\rho(H_{2}\cdots H_{n}))=(st,rq)
. Since
(s,r)
is
\mathbf{H}
-stable, Property (
15
) yields
(x,y)\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
such that
(sx^{\prime},ry^{\prime})=(sxt^{\prime}_{1},ry\rho(H_{1}))
. Altogether, it follows that
(sxe,ry\rho(H))=(st,rq)
. This concludes the proof of (
16
).

We use (
16
) to conclude Fact
9.10
. Indeed, since
(sxe,ry\rho(H))=(st,rq)
for
(x,y)\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
, if
(e,\rho(H))
is a pair of multiplicative idempotents, then we obtain
(ste,rq\rho(H))=(st,rq)
.
∎

We are ready to prove that
(st,rq)\in S
for every
(t,q)\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
. We first treat the special case when
t
is an idempotent of
N
. Then, we reuse this special case to treat the general one. Assume that
t
is an idempotent
e\in E(N)
. Hence,
(e,q)\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
and we need to prove that
(se,rq)\in S
. By definition, there are finitely many
\mathbf{H}
-products
(e,H_{1}),\dots,(e,H_{\ell})
such that
q=\rho(H_{1})+\cdots+\rho(H_{\ell})
. Consider an index
i\leq\ell
. Since
(e,H_{i})
is an
\mathbf{H}
-product and we know that
(t^{\prime},\rho(H^{\prime}))\in S
for every
(t^{\prime},H^{\prime})\in\mathbf{H}
, it follows from closure under multiplication for
S
(recall that
S
is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated) that
(e,\rho(H_{i}))\in S
. Since
e
is idempotent, it then follows from
\mathit{SF}
-closure that
(e,(\rho(H_{i}))^{\omega}+(\rho(H_{i}))^{\omega+1})\in S
. Since this holds for all
i\leq\ell
,
e
is idempotent and
(s,r)\in S
, closure under multiplication yields,

\Bigl(se,\quad r\prod_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\left((\rho(H_{i}))^{\omega}+(\rho(H_{i}))^{\omega+1}\right)\Bigr)\in S.

Let
k=\omega(R)
. For every
i\leq\ell
, we have
(e,(\rho(H_{i}))^{\omega})=(e,\rho(H_{i}^{k}))
and it is clear that
(e,H_{i}^{k})
is an
\mathbf{H}
-product since this is the case for
(e,H_{i})
. Therefore, since
(e,(\rho(H_{i}))^{\omega})
is a pair of idempotents, Fact
9.10
implies that
(st^{\prime}e,rq^{\prime}(\rho(H_{i}))^{\omega}))=(st^{\prime},rq^{\prime})
for every
(t^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
. This yields:

\Bigl(se,\quad r\prod_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\left((\rho(H_{i}))^{\omega}+(\rho(H_{i}))^{\omega+1}\right)\Bigr)=\Bigl(se,\quad r\prod_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\left(1_{R}+\rho(H_{i})\right)\Bigl),

Therefore,
(se,r\prod_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\left(1_{R}+\rho(H_{i})\right))\in S
.
Note that
q=\rho(H_{1})+\cdots+\rho(H_{\ell})\leq\prod_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\left(1_{R}+\rho(H_{i})\right)
. By closure under downset for
S
, we get
(se,rq)\in S
, which concludes the case when
t
is idempotent.

We now consider an arbitrary element
(t,q)\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
(
i.e.
,
t\in N
need not be idempotent) and show that
(st,rq)\in S
. By definition of
Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
there are finitely many
\mathbf{H}
-products
(t,H_{1}),\dots(t,H_{n})
such that
q=\rho(H_{1})+\cdots\rho(H_{n})
. Since every
(t^{\prime},H^{\prime})\in\mathbf{H}
satisfies
(t^{\prime},\rho(H^{\prime}))\in S
by hypothesis and
S
is closed under multiplication, we get
(t,\rho(H_{i}))\in S
for every
i\leq n
. Moreover, there exists a number
k\geq 1
such that
(t^{k},(\rho(H_{i}))^{k})\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
is a pair of idempotents for each
i\leq n
. In particular,
t^{k}
is an idempotent of
N
. Clearly, we have
(t^{k},q(\rho(H_{1}))^{k-1})\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
. Since
t^{k}\lx@nobreakspace\in N
is an idempotent, we obtain from the above special case that
(st^{k},rq(\rho(H_{1}))^{k-1})\in S
. Since we also have
(t,\rho(H_{1}))\in S
, it then follows from closure under multiplication that
(st^{k+1},rq(\rho(H_{1}))^{k})\in S
. Finally, since
(t^{k},(\rho(H_{1}))^{k})
is a pair of idempotents, we obtain from Fact
9.10
that
(st,rq)=(st^{k+1},rq(\rho(H_{1}))^{k})\in S
. This concludes the proof for the base case.

Inductive step:
(s,r)
is not
\mathbf{H}
-stable.
Our hypothesis yields a pair
(t,H)\in\mathbf{H}
such that the following
strict
inclusion holds:

(17)

(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H))\subsetneq(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}.

We fix this pair
(t,H)\in\mathbf{H}
for the remainder of the proof. First, we use induction on our second parameter in Lemma
9.8
to prove the following fact.

Fact 9.11
.

There exists an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{U}
of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
such that
(x,\rho(U))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cap S
for every
(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}
.

Proof.

Since
P
is a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay, Fact
4.6
implies that so is
P\setminus H
. We want to apply induction in Lemma
9.8
for the case when
P
has been replaced by
P\setminus H
. Let
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{H}\setminus\{(t,H)\}
. By hypothesis on
\mathbf{H}
, one may verify that
\mathbf{G}
is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
P\setminus H
and that
(t^{\prime},\rho(G))\in S
for every
(t^{\prime},G)\in\mathbf{G}
. Finally, it is immediate that
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{G}}\subseteq Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}
(our first induction has not increased) and
\mathbf{G}\subsetneq\mathbf{H}
(our second induction parameter has decreased). Hence, we may apply Lemma
9.8
in the case when
P,\mathbf{H}
and
(s,r)\in S
have been replaced by
P\setminus H,\mathbf{G}
and
(1_{N},1_{R})\in S
. This yields an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{U}
of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
such that
(x,\rho(U))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cap S
for every
(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}
.
∎

We fix the
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{U}
of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
given by Fact
9.11
for the remainder of the proof. We distinguish two subcases. Since
(t,H)\in\mathbf{H}
, one may verify from the definitions of
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}
and
Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
that
Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H))\subseteq Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}
. We consider two subcases depending on whether this inclusion is strict.

Subcase 1: we have the equality
Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H))=Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}
.
We use the following fact, which is proved using our hypotheses and induction on our third parameter (
i.e.
, the size of
(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
).

Fact 9.12
.

For every
(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}
, there exists an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{W}_{x,U}
of
P^{*}
such that
(y,\rho(W))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
and
(sxty,r\rho(UHW))\in S
for every
(y,W)\in\mathbf{W}_{x,U}
.

Proof.

We fix
(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}
for the proof. By definition of
\mathbf{U}
in Fact
9.11
,
(x,\rho(U))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
and
(x,\rho(U))\in S
. Hence, since
(s,r),(t,\rho(H))\in S
by hypothesis and
S
is closed under multiplication, we get
(sxt,r\rho(UH))\in S
. Moreover, it is clear that we have the inclusions
(sxt,r\rho(UH))\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\subseteq(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H))\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\subseteq(s,r)\cdot Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}
. Combined with our hypothesis in Subcase 1 (
i.e.
,
Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H))=Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}
), this yields
(sxt,r\rho(UH))\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\subseteq(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H))
. We may then use (
17
) (
i.e.
, the inclusion
(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H))\subsetneq(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
) to get the
strict
inclusion
(sxt,r\rho(UH))\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\subseteq(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
. Consequently, induction on our third parameter (
i.e.
, the size of
(s,r)\cdot Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
) in Lemma
9.8
(we consider the case when
(s,r)\in S
has been replaced by
(sxt,r\rho(UH))\in S
) yields the desired
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{W}_{x,U}
of
P^{*}
. Note that here, our first two parameters have not increased as they only depend on
\mathbf{H}
, which remains unchanged.
∎

It remains to use Fact
9.12
to conclude the proof of Subcase 1. We build our
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
as follows,

\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{U}\cup\bigcup_{(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}}\{(xty,UHW)\mid(y,W)\in\mathbf{W}_{x,U}\}.

Let us show that
\mathbf{K}
is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
P^{*}
satisfying (
14
). First, observe that for every
(t^{\prime},K)\in\mathbf{K}
, we have
K\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. This is immediate by hypothesis on
\mathbf{U}
when
(t^{\prime},K)\in\mathbf{U}
. Otherwise,
K=UHW
for
(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}
and
(y,W)\in\mathbf{W}_{x,U}
and
U,H,W\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
. Hence,
K\in\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
since
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
is closed under concatenation.

That
\mathbf{K}
is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
P^{*}
is also simple to verify since
P
is a prefix code,
H\subseteq P
and
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{W}_{x,U}
are
\eta
-pointed partitions of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
and
P^{*}
respectively. Each word
w\in P^{*}
admits a
unique
decomposition
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
with
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in P
. We partition
P^{*}
by looking at the leftmost factor belonging to
H
(if it exists).

It remains to prove that (
14
) holds. Consider
(t^{\prime},K)\in\mathbf{K}
, we show that
(t^{\prime},\rho(K))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
and
(st^{\prime},r\rho(K))\in S
. If
(t^{\prime},K)\in\mathbf{U}
, this is immediate by definition of
\mathbf{U}
in Fact
9.11
. Otherwise,
(t^{\prime},K)=(wty,UHW)
with
(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}
and
(y,W)\in\mathbf{W}_{x,U}
. By definition of
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{W}_{x,U}
, we have
(x,\rho(U)),(y,\rho(W))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
. Thus,
(xty,\rho(UHW))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
. Moreover,
(sxty,r\rho(UHW))\in S
by definition of
\mathbf{W}_{x,U}
in Fact
9.12
. This concludes the first subcase.

Subcase 2: we have the strict inclusion
Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H))\subsetneq Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}
.
Recall that our objective is to construct an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
satisfying (
14
). We begin by giving a brief overview of the construction. Consider a word
w\in P^{*}
. Since
P
is a prefix code,
w
admits a unique decomposition as a concatenation of factors in
P
. We may look at the rightmost factor in
H\subseteq P
to uniquely decompose
w
in two parts (each of them possibly empty): a prefix in
((P\setminus H)^{*}H)^{*}
and a suffix in
(P\setminus H)^{*}
. We use induction to construct
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partitions of the sets of possible prefixes and suffixes. Then, we combine them to construct an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of the whole set
P^{*}
. Actually, we already constructed a suitable
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of the possible suffixes in
(P\setminus H)^{*}
:
\mathbf{U}
(see Fact
9.11
). Hence, it remains to partition the prefixes. We do so in the following lemma, which is proved using the hypothesis of Subcase 2 and induction on our first parameter.

Fact 9.13
.

There is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{V}
of
((P\setminus H)^{*}H)^{*}
such that
(z,\rho(V))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
and
(z,\rho(V))\in S
for every
V\in\mathbf{V}
.

Proof.

Let
L=(P\setminus H)^{*}H
. Since
P
is a prefix code with bounded synchronization delay, so is
L
by Fact
4.7
. We want to apply induction in Lemma
9.8
for the case when
P
has been replaced by
L
. Doing so requires building an appropriate
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
L
and proving that one of our induction parameters has decreased.

Let
\mathbf{F}=\{(xt,UH)\mid(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}\}
. Since
\mathbf{U}
is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
(P\setminus H)^{*}
and
P
is a prefix code, one may verify that
\mathbf{F}
is an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
L=(P\setminus H)^{*}H
. Finally, given
(y,F)\in\mathbf{F}
, we have
(y,F)=(xt,UH)
for
(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}
, which means that
(y,\rho(F))=(xt,\rho(UH))\in S
since
S
is closed under multiplication. It remains to show that our induction parameters have decreased. Since
\mathbf{F}=\{(xt,UH)\mid(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}\}
and
(x,\rho(U))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
for every
(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}
(by definition of
\mathbf{U}
in Fact
9.11
), one may verify that
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{F}}\subseteq Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H))
. Hence, since
Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}\cdot(t,\rho(H))\subsetneq Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}
by hypothesis in Subcase 2, we have
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{F}}\subsetneq Q^{+}_{\mathbf{H}}
. Our first induction parameter has decreased. Altogether, it follows that we may apply Lemma
9.8
in the case when
P,\mathbf{H}
and
(s,r)\in S
have been replaced by
L,\mathbf{F}
and
(1_{N},1_{R})\in S
. This yields an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{V}
of
L^{*}=((P\setminus H)^{*}H)^{*}
such that for every
(z,V)\in\mathbf{V}
,
(z,\rho(V))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{F}}
and
(z,\rho(V))\in S
. Finally, it is clear by definition that
Q^{*}_{\mathbf{F}}\subseteq Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
. Hence, the lemma follows.
∎

We are ready to construct the
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition
\mathbf{K}
of
P^{*}
and conclude the main argument. We let
\mathbf{K}=\{(zx,VU)\mid(z,V)\in\mathbf{V}\text{ and }(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}\}
. It is immediate by definition and Fact
4.7
that
\mathbf{K}
is an
\eta
-pointed partition of
P^{*}
since
P
is a prefix code and
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{U}
are
\eta
-pointed partitions of
((P\setminus H)^{*}H)^{*}
and
(P\setminus H)^{*}
respectively (see the above discussion). Additionally, it is immediate by definition that
\mathbf{K}
is actually an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-partition of
P^{*}
(it only contains concatenations of languages in
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
). It remains to prove that
\mathbf{K}
satisfies (
14
). Let
(t^{\prime},K)\in\mathbf{K}
. By definition, there are
(z,V)\in\mathbf{V}
and
(x,U)\in\mathbf{U}
such that
(t^{\prime},K)=(zx,VU)
. By definition of
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
, we have
(x,\rho(U))(z,\rho(V))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
and
(x,\rho(U))(z,\rho(V))\in S
. Moreover,
(s,r)\in S
by hypothesis. Therefore, since both
Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
and
S
are closed under multiplication, it follows that
(t^{\prime},\rho(K))\in Q^{*}_{\mathbf{H}}
and
(st^{\prime},r\rho(K))\in S
. This completes the proof of Lemma
9.8
.
∎

We may now combine Proposition
9.5
and Proposition
9.6
to prove Theorem
9.3
. The argument is standard.

Proof of Theorem
9.3
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a finite prevariety and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a
nice
multiplicative rating map. We have to prove that
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
is the least
\mathit{SF}
-saturated subset of
N_{\mathscr{C}}\times R
for
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\rho
. We proved in Proposition
9.5
that
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]
is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated. We need to show that it is the least such set. Let
S\subseteq N_{\mathscr{C}}\times R
which is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated for
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\rho
. Proposition
9.6
yields an
\eta_{\mathscr{C}}
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-cover of
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
such that
\mathscr{P}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq S
. Since Lemma
9.2
implies that
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]\subseteq\mathscr{P}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho](\mathbf{K})
, we obtain
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})}[\eta_{\mathscr{C}},\rho]\subseteq S
, which completes the proof.
∎

10.
Covering for group input classes

We now consider separation and covering for the classes
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
when
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety. We prove that both problems are decidable when
\mathscr{G}
-separation is decidable. In this case as well, the algorithm is based on the framework introduced in Section
8
: we present a generic effective characterization of optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
. Given an arbitrary
group
prevariety
\mathscr{G}
and a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
, it describes the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]\subseteq R
. Moreover, this description is effective when
\mathscr{G}
-separation is decidable. As announced at the end of Section
8
, the characterization actually describes a more general object than the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]
. The first part of the section is devoted to defining this object. In the second part, we present and prove the characterization itself.

10.1.
Optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identities

As explained above, characterizing the optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
(when
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety) requires working with more general objects than the ones that we actually want to compute: the sets
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]\subseteq R
associated to a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
. In this case as well, we look at sets which are strongly related to the “nested optimal imprints”
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]\subseteq 2^{R}
associated to the auxiliary rating maps
\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]:2^{A^{*}}\to 2^{R}
. Yet, since we are dealing with input classes that are
group prevarieties
, it will not be necessary to consider the whole set: a single special element of
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]\subseteq 2^{R}
suffices. Let us first define it. The definition makes sense for
all rating maps
: given an arbitrary rating map
\tau:2^{A^{*}}\to Q
, we identify a special element of
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\subseteq Q
.

The definition is based on a simple idea: a group prevariety
\mathscr{G}
does not contain any finite language. In particular,
\{\varepsilon\}\not\in\mathscr{G}
. Hence, given a rating map
\tau:2^{A^{*}}\to Q
, the optimal
\tau
-imprint for
\mathscr{G}
on the singleton
\{\varepsilon\}
,
i.e.
,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\{\varepsilon\},\tau]
, is an important object. This leads to the following definition.

Optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identities.
Let
\mathscr{G}
be a lattice and let
\tau:2^{A^{*}}\to Q
be a rating map. We call
\mathscr{G}
-identity
any language in
\mathscr{G}
containing
\varepsilon
. An
optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identity for
\tau
is a
\mathscr{G}
-identity
L
such that for every
\mathscr{G}
-identity
L^{\prime}
, we have
\tau(L)\leq\tau(L^{\prime})
. In practice, we use this notion when
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety, but this is not required for the definition. As expected, optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identities for
\tau
always exist.

Lemma 10.1
.

For any lattice
\mathscr{G}
and rating map
\tau:2^{A^{*}}\to Q
, there exists an optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identity for
\tau
.

Proof.

Let
U=\{\tau(L)\mid L\in\mathscr{G}\text{ and }\varepsilon\in L\}=\{\tau(L)\mid L\text{ is a $\mathscr{G}$-identity }\}
. Clearly,
U
is nonempty:
\tau(A^{*})\in U
since
A^{*}\in\mathscr{G}
, as
\mathscr{G}
is a lattice. For every
q\in U
, fix an arbitrary
\mathscr{G}
-identity
L_{q}
such that
q=\tau(L_{q})
and let
L=\bigcap_{q\in U}L_{q}
. Since
\mathscr{G}
is a lattice, we have
L\in\mathscr{G}
. Moreover,
\varepsilon\in L
by definition. Since
L\subseteq L_{q}
for all
q\in U
, it follows that
\tau(L)\leq q
for every
q\in U
. By definition of
U
, this implies that
\tau(L)\leq\tau(L^{\prime})
for every
\mathscr{G}
-identity
L^{\prime}
. Hence,
L
is an optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identity for
\tau
.
∎

We complete the definition with a second notion, which is the counterpart of optimal imprints in this context. By definition, all optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identities for
\tau
have the same image under
\tau
. Hence, this image (in
Q
) is a canonical object for
