\mathscr{G}
and
\tau
. We write it
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\in Q
. In other words,
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]=\tau(K)
for every optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identity
K
for
\tau
. We now connect this object with optimal imprints.

Lemma 10.2
.

If
\mathscr{G}
is a lattice and
\tau:2^{A^{*}}\to Q
is a rating map,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\{\varepsilon\},\tau]=\mathord{\downarrow_{Q}}\{\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\}
.

Proof.

For the left to right inclusion, let
L
be an optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identity for
\tau
. By definition, we have
L\in\mathscr{G}
,
\varepsilon\in L
and
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]=\tau(L)
. Clearly,
\mathbf{K}=\{L\}
is a
\mathscr{G}
-cover of
\{\varepsilon\}
. It follows that
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\{\varepsilon\},\tau]\subseteq\mathscr{I}[\tau](\mathbf{K})=\mathord{\downarrow_{Q}}\{\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\}
. Conversely, let
\mathbf{H}
be an optimal
\mathscr{G}
-cover of
\{\varepsilon\}
for
\tau
. There exists
H\in\mathbf{H}
such that
\varepsilon\in H
. Hence,
\{H\}
is also an optimal
\mathscr{G}
-cover of
\{\varepsilon\}
for
\tau
and it follows that
\mathord{\downarrow_{Q}}\{\tau(H)\}=\mathscr{I}[\tau](\{H\})=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\{\varepsilon\},\tau]
. Finally, since
H\in\mathscr{G}
and
\varepsilon\in H
, we have
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\leq\tau(H)
, which yields
\mathord{\downarrow_{Q}}\{\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\}\subseteq\mathord{\downarrow_{Q}}\{\tau(H)\}
. Altogether, we obtain
\mathord{\downarrow_{Q}}\{\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\{\varepsilon\},\tau]
, as desired.
∎

We complete the definition with a key property. When
\tau
is a
nice
rating map, the element
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\in Q
can be specified in terms of
\mathscr{G}
-separation.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a lattice and
\tau:2^{A^{*}}\to Q
be a nice rating map. Let
S\subseteq Q
be the set of all elements
s\in Q
such that
\{\varepsilon\}
is not
\mathscr{G}
-separable from
\tau_{*}^{-1}(s)
. Then,
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]=\sum_{s\in S}s
.

Proof.

First, let us prove that
\sum_{s\in S}s\leq\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]
. This boils down to proving that
s\leq\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]
for every
s\in S
. By definition of
S
, we know that
\{\varepsilon\}
is not
\mathscr{G}
-separable from
\tau_{*}^{-1}(s)
. Let
L
be an optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identity for
\tau
. By definition, we have
L\in\mathscr{G}
,
\varepsilon\in L
and
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]=\tau(L)
. Since
\{\varepsilon\}
is not
\mathscr{G}
-separable from
\tau_{*}^{-1}(s)
,
L\in\mathscr{G}
cannot separate
\{\varepsilon\}
and
\tau_{*}^{-1}(s)
, and therefore
L\cap\tau_{*}^{-1}(s)\neq\emptyset
. It follows that
s\leq\tau(L)
,
i.e.
, that
s\leq\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]
.

Conversely, we prove that
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\leq\sum_{s\in S}s
. For every
q\in Q\setminus S
, we know that
\{\varepsilon\}
is
\mathscr{G}
-separable from
\tau_{*}^{-1}(q)
. Hence, we get
H_{q}\in\mathscr{G}
such that
\varepsilon\in H_{q}
and
H_{q}\cap\tau_{*}^{-1}(q)=\emptyset
. Let
H=\bigcap_{q\in Q\setminus S}H_{q}
. Since
\mathscr{G}
is a lattice, we have
H\in\mathscr{G}
. Moreover,
\varepsilon\in H
by definition. Therefore,
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\leq\tau(H)
. Since
\tau
is
nice
, there are finitely many words
w_{1},\dots,w_{k}\in H
such that
\tau(H)=\tau(w_{1})+\cdots+\tau(w_{k})
. Finally, it follows from the definition of
H
that
\tau(w_{i})\not\in Q\setminus S
for every
i\leq k
. In other words, we have
\tau(w_{i})\in S
for every
i\leq k
and we obtain that
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\leq\tau(w_{1})+\cdots+\tau(w_{k})\leq\sum_{s\in S}s
, as desired.
∎

In particular when
\mathscr{G}
has decidable separation, Lemma
10.3
yields an algorithm taking a
nice multiplicative rating map

\tau:2^{A^{*}}\to Q
as input and computing the element
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\in Q
.

Corollary 10.4
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a lattice prevariety with decidable separation. Given a nice multiplicative rating map
\tau:2^{A^{*}}\to Q
as input, the element
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\tau]\in Q
is computable.

Application to
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
-covering.
We now explain how these notions are used in the context of
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
-covering for a group prevariety
\mathscr{G}
. Consider a prevariety
\mathscr{D}
containing
\mathscr{G}
and which is closed under concatenation. Intuitively,
\mathscr{D}
is meant to be a class that has been built from
\mathscr{G}
using an operator. In practice, we shall use the case when
\mathscr{D}=\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
. The high levels ideas outlined in Section
8
suggest that given a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
, characterizing the optimal
\rho
-imprint for
\mathscr{D}
,
i.e.
,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]\subseteq R
, involves working with the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]\subseteq 2^{R}
defined from the auxiliary rating map
\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]:2^{A^{*}}\to 2^{R}
. Yet, because
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety and
\mathscr{D}
is closed under concatenation, partial information on
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]
suffices. As we prove below, it is enough to consider the
single element

\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]\in 2^{R}
(which belongs to
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]
by Lemma
10.2
).

First, observe that regardless of our hypotheses on
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{D}
, this set is directly connected to the object that we truly want to characterize: the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]\subseteq R
. Indeed, by definition, we have
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]=\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho](L)=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[L,\rho]
where
L\in\mathscr{G}
is an optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identity for
\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
. Hence Fact
8.5
implies that,

\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[L,\rho]\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[A^{*},\rho]=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho].

It turns out that when
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety and
\mathscr{D}
is closed under concatenation,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
is
characterized
by its subset
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]
. That is, one may compute the former from the latter.

Proposition 10.5
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a group prevariety and
\mathscr{D}
be a prevariety closed under concatenation such that
\mathscr{G}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
. Let
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a multiplicative rating map. Then,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
is the least subset
S\subseteq R
containing
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]
and satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

Trivial elements:
For every
w\in A^{*}
, we have
\rho(w)\in S
.

(2)

Closure under downset:
We have
\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}S=S
.

(3)

Closure under multiplication:
For every
q,r\in S
, we have
qr\in S
.

Clearly, Proposition
10.5
yields a least fixpoint procedure for computing the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
from the set
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]
. This is exactly how we handle optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
below. Rather than directly characterizing the sets
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]\subseteq R
for all nice multiplicative rating maps
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
, we instead characterize the sets
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]\subseteq R
, which
carry more information
by Proposition
10.5
. Before we present the characterization, let us prove Proposition
10.5
.

Proof of Proposition
10.5
.

Let
S\subseteq R
be the least subset of
R
containing
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]
and satisfying the three conditions in Proposition
10.5
. We first prove that
S\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
. This is immediate. Indeed, as seen above, we have
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
. Also,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
contains all trivial elements: clearly,
\rho(w)\in\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[A^{*},\rho]=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
for every
w\in A^{*}
. Moreover,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
is closed under downset since it is an imprint. Finally, since
\mathscr{D}
is a prevariety, Lemma
8.7
implies that
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
is closed under multiplication. Altogether, we get
S\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
as desired.

We turn to the inclusion
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]\subseteq S
. We build a
\mathscr{D}
-cover
\mathbf{K}
of
A^{*}
such that
\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq S
. Since
\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]\subseteq\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})
by definition, this yields the desired result. Let
L\subseteq A^{*}
be an optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identity for
\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]
. By definition,
L\in\mathscr{G}
,
\varepsilon\in L
and
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]=\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho](L)=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[L,\rho]
. We now use the following lemma to build a special cover of
A^{*}
.

Lemma 10.6
.

There exists a cover
\mathbf{U}
of
A^{*}
such that each
U\in\mathbf{U}
satisfies
U=La_{1}L\cdots a_{\ell}L
where
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in A
(if
\ell=0
, then
U=L
).

Proof.

With every word
w=a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}\in A^{*}
, we associate the language
U_{w}=La_{1}L\cdots a_{\ell}L
(in particular,
U_{\varepsilon}=L
). Since
L\in\mathscr{G}
, Proposition
2.4
yields a
\mathscr{G}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to G
recognizing
L
. By hypothesis on
\mathscr{G}
, Lemma
3.5
implies that
G
is a group. We let
k=|G|
and
\mathbf{U}=\{U_{w}\mid w\in A^{*}\text{ and }|w|\leq k\}
. All languages in
\mathbf{U}
have the desired form. Hence, it suffices to verify that
\mathbf{U}
is a cover of
A^{*}
. We fix
v\in A^{*}
and exhibit
U\in\mathbf{U}
such that
v\in U
. It can be verified using a pumping argument that there exist
\ell\leq k
,
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in A
and
v_{0},\dots,v_{\ell}\in A^{*}
such that
v=v_{0}a_{1}v_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}v_{\ell}
and
\eta(v_{0}a_{1}v_{1}\cdots a_{i}v_{i})=\eta(v_{0}a_{1}v_{1}\cdots a_{i})
for every
i\leq\ell
. Since
G
is a group, this yields
\eta(v_{i})=1_{G}
for all
i\leq\ell
. Hence, since
\eta
recognizes
L
and
\varepsilon\in L
, we get
v_{i}\in L
for every
i\leq\ell
. Thus
v\in U_{w}
for
w=a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}
. Finally, we have
U_{w}\in\mathbf{U}
since
|w|=\ell\leq k
. This completes the proof.
∎

Let
\mathbf{H}
be an optimal
\mathscr{D}
-cover of
L
for
\rho
. By definition, we have
\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{H})=\mathscr{I}_{\mathscr{D}}[L,\rho]=\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]
. Consider the cover
\mathbf{U}
of
A^{*}
given by Lemma
10.6
. Let
U\in\mathbf{U}
. By definition
U=La_{1}L\cdots a_{n}L
for
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
. Let
\mathbf{K}_{U}=\{H_{0}a_{1}H_{1}\cdots a_{n}H_{n}\mid H_{0},\dots,H_{n}\in\mathbf{H}\}
. Since
\mathbf{H}
is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover of
L
and
\mathscr{D}
is closed under concatenation,
\mathbf{K}_{U}
is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover of
U
. Finally, let
\mathbf{K}=\bigcup_{U\in\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{K}_{U}
. Since
\mathbf{U}
is a cover of
A^{*}
and
\mathbf{K}_{U}
is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover of
U
for every
U\in\mathbf{U}
, it is immediate that
\mathbf{K}
is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover of
A^{*}
.

It remains to prove that
\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq S
. Since
S
is closed under downset, it suffices to prove that
\rho(K)\in S
for every
K\in\mathbf{K}
. By definition, there exist
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
and
H_{0},\dots,H_{n}\in\mathbf{H}
such that
K=H_{0}a_{1}H_{1}\cdots a_{n}H_{n}
. Hence, it suffices to prove that
\rho(H_{0}a_{1}H_{1}\cdots a_{n}H_{n})\in S
. By definition of
\mathbf{H}
, we have
\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{H})=\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]
. Therefore,
\rho(H_{i})\in\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]
for every
i\leq n
. Since
S
contains
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathscr{D}}[\rho]]
, this yields
\rho(H_{i})\in S
for every
i\leq n
. Moreover, since
S
contains the trivial elements, we have
\rho(a_{i})\in S
for every
i\leq n
. Finally, since
S
is closed under multiplication, we obtain
\rho(H_{0}a_{1}H_{1}\cdots a_{n}H_{n})\in S
, which completes the proof.
∎

10.2.
Characterization

Let us first present the characterization. Consider an arbitrary group prevariety
\mathscr{G}
and an arbitrary multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
. We define special subsets of
R
, which we call
\mathit{SF}
-complete
for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
. The definition is based on an auxiliary nice multiplicative rating map
\mu_{\rho,S}:2^{A^{*}}\to 2^{R}
, which we associate to every subset
S\subseteq R
. Its rating algebra is
(2^{R},\cup,\cdot)
(the multiplication on
2^{R}
is obtained by lifting the multiplication on
R
to subsets). Since we are defining a
nice
multiplicative rating map, it suffices to specify the image of each letter
a\in A
. We let,

\mu_{\rho,S}(a)=\{s\rho(a)s^{\prime}\mid s,s^{\prime}\in S\}\in 2^{R}.

Observe that by definition, we have
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\mu_{\rho,S}]\subseteq R
for each set
S\subseteq R
. Now, consider an arbitrary subset
S\subseteq R
. We say that
S
is
\mathit{SF}
-complete for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
when it satisfies the following properties:

(1)

Closure under downset:

\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}S=S
.

(2)

Closure under multiplication:
For every
q,r\in S
, we have
qr\in S
.

(3)

\mathscr{G}
-operation:
We have
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\mu_{\rho,S}]\subseteq S
.

(4)

\mathit{SF}
-closure.
For every
r\in S
, we have
r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1}\in S
.

Remark 10.7
.

The definition does not explicitly require that an
\mathit{SF}
-complete subset
S\subseteq R
for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
contains some trivial elements. Yet, this is a consequence of
\mathscr{G}
-operation. Indeed, we have
\mu_{\rho,S}(\varepsilon)=\{1_{R}\}
(this is the multiplicative neutral element of
2^{R}
). Thus,
1_{R}\in\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\mu_{\rho,S}]\subseteq S
by definition.

With this definition in hand, we may state the main theorem of this section. When
\rho
is nice,
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]
is the least
\mathit{SF}
-complete subset of
R
.

Theorem 10.8
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a group prevariety and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
a
nice
multiplicative rating map. Then,
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]
is the least
\mathit{SF}
-complete subset of
R
for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
.

When
\mathscr{G}
-separation is decidable, Theorem
10.8
yields a least fixpoint procedure for computing
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]
from a nice multiplicative rating map
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
. The computation starts from the empty set and saturates it with the operations in the definition of
\mathit{SF}
-complete subsets. It is clear that we may implement downset, multiplication and
\mathit{SF}
-closure. Moreover, we may compute
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\mu_{\rho,S}]
from a set
S\subseteq R
by Corollary
10.4
since
\mathscr{G}
-separation is decidable. Eventually, the computation reaches a fixpoint and it is straightforward to verify that this set is the least
\mathit{SF}
-complete subset of
R
,
i.e.
,
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]
by Theorem
10.8
. One may then use a second least fixpoint procedure provided by Proposition
10.5
to compute the set
\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]\subseteq R
from
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]
.

Consequently, it follows from Proposition
8.8
that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
-covering is decidable if
\mathscr{G}
-separation is decidable. As before, the result can be lifted to separation for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
using Lemma
2.2
. Altogether, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 10.9
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a group prevariety with decidable separation. Then, separation and covering are decidable for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
.

Corollary
10.9
has three important applications: the group prevarieties
MOD
(the modulo languages),
AMT
(the alphabet modulo testable languages) and
GR
(all group languages). Indeed, since it is known that the three of them have decidable separation (see
e.g.
,
Place & Zeitoun 2023
), we obtain the decidability of covering for the classes
\mathit{SF}(\textup{MOD})
,
\mathit{SF}(\textup{AMT})
and
\mathit{SF}(\textup{GR})
.

We now concentrate on the proof of Theorem
10.8
. In this case as well, we present two independent statements corresponding respectively to soundness and completeness. Let us start with the former. The argument is based on Proposition
9.5
, which addresses soundness for the characterization of optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
when
\mathscr{C}
is a
finite
prevariety.

Proposition 10.10

(Soundness)
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a group prevariety of group languages and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a multiplicative rating map. Then,
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]\subseteq R
is
\mathit{SF}
-complete for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
.

Proof.

For the sake of avoiding clutter, let
S=\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]
. We start with a preliminary result.

Lemma 10.11
.

There exists a finite group prevariety
\mathscr{H}
that satisfies
\mathscr{H}\subseteq\mathscr{G}
and such that
S=\{r\in R\mid(1_{G},r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta_{\mathscr{H}},\rho]\}
where
\eta_{\mathscr{H}}:A^{*}\to G
is the canonical
\mathscr{H}
-morphism.

Before we prove Lemma
10.11
, let us use it to complete the main argument. Note that Proposition
9.5
implies that
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta_{\mathscr{H}},\rho]\subseteq G\times R
is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated for
\eta_{\mathscr{H}}
and
\rho
. We shall use this property multiple times. We show that
S
is
\mathit{SF}
-complete for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
. That
S
is closed under downset, multiplication and
\mathit{SF}
-closure is immediate from Lemma
10.11
(more precisely, it follows from the equality
S=\{r\in R\mid(1_{G},r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta_{\mathscr{H}},\rho]\}
and the fact that
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta_{\mathscr{H}},\rho]
satisfies the three corresponding properties in the definition of
\mathit{SF}
-saturated sets). Hence, we concentrate on
\mathscr{G}
-operation. Given
s\in\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\mu_{\rho,S}]
, we prove that
s\in S
. By hypothesis in Lemma
10.11
, we have
\eta_{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(1_{G})\in\mathscr{H}\subseteq\mathscr{G}
. Moreover, it is clear that
\varepsilon\in\eta_{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(1_{G})
. Therefore, we obtain
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\mu_{\rho,S}]\subseteq\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta_{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(1_{G}))
. This yields
w\in\eta_{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(1_{G})
such that
s\in\mu_{\rho,S}(w)
. There are now two cases. First, if
w=\varepsilon
, then
\mu_{\rho,S}(\varepsilon)=\{1_{R}\}
which yields
s=1_{R}
. Clearly, we have
(1_{G},1_{R})\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta_{\mathscr{H}},\rho]
(this is a trivial element), hence we obtain
s=1_{R}\in S
by Lemma
10.11
. Assume now that
w\in A^{+}
and let
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
be the letters such that
w=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}
. By definition of
\mu_{\rho,S}
, the fact that
s\in\mu_{\rho,S}(w)
yields
s_{1},\dots,s_{n},t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in S
such that
s=s_{1}\rho(a_{1})t_{1}\cdots s_{n}\rho(a_{n})t_{n}
. Clearly, we have
(\eta_{\mathscr{H}}(a_{i}),\rho(a_{i}))\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta_{\mathscr{H}},\rho]
for every
i\leq n
(this is a trivial element). Also, Lemma
10.11
yields
(1_{G},s_{i}),(1_{G},t_{i})\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta_{\mathscr{H}},\rho]
for every
i\leq n
. Altogether, since
\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta_{\mathscr{H}},\rho]
is closed under multiplication, we get
(\eta_{\mathscr{H}}(w),s)=(\eta_{\mathscr{H}}(a_{1}\cdots a_{n}),s)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta_{\mathscr{H}},\rho]
. Finally, since
w\in\eta_{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(1_{G})
, we get
(1_{G},s)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta_{\mathscr{H}},\rho]
and Lemma
10.11
yields
s\in S
, as desired.

It remains to prove Lemma
10.11
. We first define
\mathscr{H}
. Let
L\in\mathscr{G}
be an optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identity for
\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]
. By definition,
\varepsilon\in L
and
S=\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]=\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho](L)
. Let
\mathbf{K}
be an optimal
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
-cover of
L
for
\rho
, so that
\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})=\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[L,\rho]=\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho](L)=S
. By definition of
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
, there exists a
finite
set of languages
\mathbf{H}\subseteq\mathscr{G}
such that every language
K\in\mathbf{K}\subseteq\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
is built from the languages in
\mathbf{H}
and the singletons
\{a\}
for
a\in A
using only concatenation and Boolean combinations. It follows from Proposition
3.2
that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
is a prevariety. Therefore, Proposition
2.4
yields a
\mathscr{G}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to G
all languages in
\{L\}\cup\mathbf{H}
. Moreover, since
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety, Lemma
3.5
implies that
G
is a group. We write
\mathscr{H}
for the class of all languages recognized by
\eta
. That is,
\mathscr{H}=\{\eta^{-1}(F)\mid F\subseteq G\}
. One may verify that
\mathscr{H}
is a finite group prevariety such that
\mathscr{H}\subseteq\mathscr{G}
. Moreover,
\eta
is an
\mathscr{H}
-morphism that recognizes every language in
\mathscr{H}
. Hence,
\eta
is the canonical
\mathscr{H}
-morphism by Lemma
2.5
.

It remains to prove that
S=\{r\in R\mid(1_{G},r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta,\rho]\}
. We start with the left to right inclusion. Let
r\in S
. By definition,
S=\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]
. Hence, since
\eta^{-1}(1_{G})\in\mathscr{G}
and
\varepsilon\in\eta^{-1}(1_{G})
, we have
r\in\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho](\eta^{-1}(1_{G}))=\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\eta^{-1}(1_{G}),\rho]
. Finally, since
\mathscr{H}\subseteq\mathscr{G}
, this implies by Fact
8.5
that
r\in\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta^{-1}(1_{G}),\rho]
, which exactly says that
(1_{G},r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta,\rho]
, as desired. We turn to the converse inclusion. Let
r\in R
such that
(1_{G},r)\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta,\rho]
. We show that
r\in S
. By definition
r\in\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[\eta^{-1}(1_{G}),\rho]
. Moreover, since
\eta
recognizes
L
and we have
\varepsilon\in L
, we have
\eta^{-1}(1_{G})\subseteq L
. Therefore, Fact
8.5
yields
r\in\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})}[L,\rho]
. Moreover, it is immediate by definition of
\mathscr{H}
that every
K\in\mathbf{K}
belongs to
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})
. Hence,
\mathbf{K}
is an
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{H})
-cover of
L
and we get
r\in\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})=S
. This completes the proof.
∎

We turn to completeness. The argument is based on Proposition
9.6
, the direction addressing completeness for the characterization of optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
when
\mathscr{C}
is a
finite
prevariety.

Proposition 10.12

(Completeness)
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a prevariety of group languages and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a nice multiplicative rating map. Let
S\subseteq R
be
\mathit{SF}
-complete for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
. Then, there exists a language
L\in\mathscr{G}
such that
\varepsilon\in L
, as well as an
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
-cover
\mathbf{K}
of
L
such that
\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq S
.

Proof.

We start with a preliminary lemma. It is based on the hypothesis that
S
is
\mathit{SF}
-complete for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
. We need it in order to apply Proposition
9.6
.

Lemma 10.13
.

There exist a
\mathscr{G}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to G
and a set
S^{\prime}\subseteq G\times R
which is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated for
\eta
and
\rho
, and such that
S=\{r\mid(1_{G},r)\in S^{\prime}\}
.

We first apply Lemma
10.13
to complete the proof of Proposition
10.12
. We define
L=\eta^{-1}(1_{G})
. It is clear that
\varepsilon\in L
and
L\in\mathscr{G}
since
\eta
is a
\mathscr{G}
-morphism. Moreover, since
S^{\prime}
is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated for
\eta
and
\rho
, Proposition
9.6
yields an
\eta
-pointed
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
-cover
\mathbf{H}
of
A^{*}
such that
\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{H})\subseteq S^{\prime}
. We define
\mathbf{K}=\{K\mid(1_{G},K)\in\mathbf{H}\}
. By definition,
\mathbf{K}
is an
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
-cover of
L=\eta^{-1}(1_{G})
. Moreover, since
S=\{r\mid(1_{G},r)\in S^{\prime}\}
and
\mathscr{P}[\eta,\rho](\mathbf{H})\subseteq S^{\prime}
, it is immediate from the definition that
\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq S
, which completes the proof.

It remains to prove Lemma
10.13
. Let us first define
\eta
. We let
L
be an optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identity for the nice multiplicative rating map
\mu_{\rho,S}:2^{A^{*}}\to 2^{R}
. That is, we have
L\in\mathscr{G}
,
\varepsilon\in L
and
\mu_{\rho,S}(L)=\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\mu_{\rho,S}]
. Proposition
2.4
yields a
\mathscr{G}
-morphism
\eta:A^{*}\to G
recognizing
L
. By hypothesis on
\mathscr{G}
, Lemma
3.5
implies that
G
is a group. Also since
\varepsilon\in L
and
L
is recognized by
\eta
, it is immediate that
\eta^{-1}(1_{G})\subseteq L
. Finally, we define
S^{\prime}\subseteq G\times R
as the following set:

S^{\prime}=\{(1_{G},s)\mid s\in S\}\cup\{(t,r)\mid r\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(t))\}.

Let us first prove that
S=\{r\mid(1_{G},r)\in S^{\prime}\}
. The left to right inclusion is immediate by definition: if
r\in S
, then
(1_{G},r)\in S^{\prime}
. We turn to the converse inclusion. Let
r\in R
such that
(1_{G},r)\in S^{\prime}
. By definition of
S^{\prime}
, either
r\in S
or
r\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(1_{G}))
. In the former case, we are done. Hence, we assume that
r\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(1_{G}))
. By definition,
\eta^{-1}(1_{G})\subseteq L
(
L
is recognized by
\eta
and
\varepsilon\in L
). Hence, since
\mu_{\rho,S}(L)=\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\mu_{\rho,S}]
we get
r\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\mu_{\rho,S}]
. Finally, since
S
is
\mathit{SF}
-complete for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
, we have
\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\mu_{\rho,S}]\subseteq S
and we get
r\in S
, as desired.

It remains to prove that
S^{\prime}
is
\mathit{SF}
-saturated for
\eta
and
\rho
. This involves four properties. Let us start with trivial elements. Consider
w\in A^{*}
, we show that
(\eta(w),\rho(w))\in S^{\prime}
. By definition of
S^{\prime}
, it suffices to prove that
\rho(w)\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(\eta(w)))
. Clearly,
w\in\eta^{-1}(\eta(w))
. Hence, it now suffices to prove that
\rho(w)\in\mu_{\rho,S}(w)
. If
w=\varepsilon
, then
\rho(w)=1_{R}
and
\mu_{\rho,S}(\varepsilon)=\{1_{R}\}
(this is the neutral element of
2^{R}
). Thus, it is immediate that
\rho(w)\in\mu_{\rho,S}(w)
. Assume now that
w\in A^{+}
. We have
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
such that
w=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}
. Since
S
is
\mathit{SF}
-complete, we have
1_{R}\in S
(see Remark
10.7
). Hence, we get
\rho(a_{i})\in S\cdot\{\rho(a_{i})\}\cdot S=\mu_{\rho,S}(a_{i})
for every
i\leq n
. It then follows that
\rho(w)\in\mu_{\rho,S}(w)
which concludes this case. We turn to downset. Consider
(t,r)\in S^{\prime}
and
q\leq r
. We show that
(t,q)\in S^{\prime}
. By definition, there are two possible cases. First, it may happen that
t=1_{G}
and
r\in S
. In that case,
q\in S
since
\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}S=S
(
S
is complete) and we get that
(1_{G},q)\in S^{\prime}
by definition. Otherwise,
r\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(t))
which yields
q\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(t))
as well and we get
(t,q)\in S^{\prime}
, concluding the proof for downset.

We turn to closure under multiplication. Let
(s,q),(t,r)\in S^{\prime}
. We have to show that
(st,qr)\in S^{\prime}
. There are several cases. Assume first that
s=t=1_{G}
. In that case, we proved above that
q,r\in S
. Since
S
is complete, this implies that
qr\in S
and we obtain that
(st,qr)=(1_{G},qr)\in S^{\prime}
. We now assume that either
s
or
t
is distinct from
1_{G}
for the remainder of this case. If both
s
and
t
are distinct from
1_{G}
, we have
q\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(s))
and
r\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(t))
by definition of
S^{\prime}
. It follows that
qr\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(s)\eta^{-1}(t))
. Since
\eta^{-1}(s)\eta^{-1}(t)\subseteq\eta^{-1}(st)
, this yields
qr\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(st))
and we get
(st,qr)\in S^{\prime}
. Finally, we handle the case when
s=1_{G}
and
t\neq 1_{G}
(the symmetrical case is left to the reader). Since
S=S^{\prime}(1_{G})
, the hypothesis that
t=1_{G}
yields
q\in S
. Moreover, we have
r\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(t))
since
t\neq 1_{G}
. Note that
t\neq 1_{G}
also implies that
\eta^{-1}(t)\subseteq A^{+}
. Thus, since
SS\subseteq S
(
S
is complete), one may verify from the definition of
\mu_{\rho,S}
that
S\cdot\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(t))\subseteq\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(t))
. Thus, since
q\in S
and
r\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(t))
, we obtain that
qr\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(t))
. Finally,
1_{G}t=t
. Thus,
qr\in\mathord{\downarrow_{R}}\mu_{\rho,S}(\eta^{-1}(1_{G}t))
which yields
(1_{G}t,qr)\in S^{\prime}
, concluding the proof.

It remains to prove that
S^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathit{SF}
-closure. Since
G
is a group,
1_{G}
is the only idempotent of
G
. Hence, we have to show that for
r\in R
such that
(1_{G},r)\in S^{\prime}
, we have
(1_{G},r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1})\in S^{\prime}
. Since
S=\{r\mid(1_{G},r)\in S^{\prime}\}
, we know that
r\in S
. Hence, since
S
is
\mathit{SF}
-complete, we get
r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1}\in S
which implies
(1_{G},r^{\omega}+r^{\omega+1})\in S^{\prime}
since
S=\{r\mid(1_{G},r)\in S^{\prime}\}
. This completes the proof.
∎

We may now combine Proposition
10.10
and Proposition
10.12
to prove Theorem
10.8
.

Proof of Theorem
10.8
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a group prevariety and
\rho:2^{A^{*}}\to R
be a nice multiplicative rating map. We prove that
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]
is the least
\mathit{SF}
-complete subset of
R
for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
. Proposition
10.10
implies that
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]
is
\mathit{SF}
-complete. We have to show that it is the least such set.

Let
S\subseteq R
be
\mathit{SF}
-complete for
\mathscr{G}
and
\rho
. We prove that
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]\subseteq S
. Proposition
10.12
yields
L\in\mathscr{G}
such that
\varepsilon\in L
and an
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
-cover
\mathbf{K}
of
L
such that
\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq S
. By definition of optimal
\mathscr{G}
-identities, we have
\mathbb{i}_{\mathscr{G}}[\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho]]\subseteq\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho](L)
. Hence, it suffices to show that
\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho](L)\subseteq S
. By definition, we have
\xi_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[\rho](L)=\mathscr{I}_{\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})}[L,\rho]\subseteq\mathscr{I}[\rho](\mathbf{K})\subseteq S
. This concludes the proof.
∎

11.
Conclusion

We investigated the star-free closure operator
\mathscr{C}\mapsto\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
applied prevarieties or regular languages. First, we presented several equivalent ways of defining
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
, including a generic algebraic characterization that generalizes earlier results
Schützenberger 1965
;
Straubing 1979
;
Pin et al. 1988
. It implies that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
-
membership
is decidable when
\mathscr{C}
-
separation
is decidable. A key proof ingredient for all these results is an alternative definition of star-free closure: the operator
\mathscr{C}\mapsto\mathit{SD}(\mathscr{C})
, which we prove to coincide with star-free closure. This correspondence generalizes the work of Schützenberger
Schützenberger 1975
who introduced a single class
\mathit{SD}
(
i.e.
,
\mathit{SD}(\textup{ST})
) corresponding to the star-free languages (
i.e.
,
\mathit{SF}(\textup{ST})
). Moreover, we presented two generic logical characterizations of star-free closure. The first one is based on first-order logic and generalizes the work of McNaughton and Papert on the star-free languages
McNaughton & Papert 1971
: we have
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{FO}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathscr{C}})
for every prevariety
\mathscr{C}
. The second one is based on linear temporal logic and generalizes Kamp’s theorem
Kamp 1968
: we have
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{LTL}(\mathscr{C})=\textup{LTLP}(\mathscr{C})
for every prevariety
\mathscr{C}
. Finally, we gave two generic characterizations of optimal imprints for
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
, for two particular kinds of prevariety
\mathscr{C}
: the
finite
prevarieties and the
group
prevarieties. They imply that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{C})
has decidable separation and covering when
\mathscr{C}
is a finite prevariety and that
\mathit{SF}(\mathscr{G})
has decidable separation and covering when
\mathscr{G}
is a group prevariety with decidable separation.
